383. Ransom Note Easy

LeetCode · cf/Ransom_Note.cpp

// orz
// 383. Ransom Note · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given two strings ransomNote and magazine, return true if ransomNote can be
// built from the letters of magazine, and false otherwise. each letter of
// magazine can be used only once in ransomNote.
// Example 1: ransomNote = "a", magazine = "b"     -> false
// Example 2: ransomNote = "aa", magazine = "ab"   -> false
// Example 3: ransomNote = "aa", magazine = "aab"  -> true
// Constraints: 1 <= ransomNote.length, magazine.length <= 10^5,
//              both are lowercase English letters only
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    for every letter c: (number of c in ransomNote) <= (number of c in magazine).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every letter of ransomNote, scan magazine for a copy that is not used yet
//    and mark it used. if some letter finds no copy, false.
//    O(n * m), 10^5 * 10^5 = 10^10. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it searches for positions, but the positions never matter. two copies of 'a'
//    in magazine are the same 'a'. only how many of each letter there are matters.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    a letter is just a count. 26 counters, one per letter, replace the search:
//    count the magazine, then spend the counts on the note.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : cnt[c] = how many times letter c is in magazine.
//    step 2 : walk the note. for each letter c, spend one: cnt[c]--.
//             if cnt[c] goes below 0, the magazine ran out of c, return false.
//    step 3 : every letter got paid for, return true.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after step 2, cnt[c] = (c in magazine) - (c in note so far). it goes below 0
//    exactly when the note needs more c than the magazine has, which is the
//    statement in 1 failing.
//    time O(n + m), extra space O(26).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    bool canConstruct(string ransomNote, string magazine) {
        ll n = ransomNote.size() ;
        ll m = magazine.size() ;
        // step 1 : cnt[c] stores how many times letter c is in the magazine
        vector < ll > cnt(26 , 0) ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < m ; i++)
        {
            cnt[magazine[i] - 'a']++ ;
        }
        // step 2 : spend one copy for every letter of the note
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            cnt[ransomNote[i] - 'a']-- ;
            if(cnt[ransomNote[i] - 'a'] < 0)
            {
                return false ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 3
        return true ;
    }
};
205. Isomorphic Strings Easy

LeetCode · cf/Isomorphic_Strings.cpp

// orz
// 205. Isomorphic Strings · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given two strings s and t, return true if they are isomorphic.
// s and t are isomorphic if the characters of s can be replaced to get t:
// every occurrence of a character is replaced by the same character, the order
// stays, no two different characters may map to the same character, and a
// character may map to itself.
// Example 1: s = "egg", t = "add"       -> true
// Example 2: s = "foo", t = "bar"       -> false
// Example 3: s = "paper", t = "title"   -> true
// Constraints: 1 <= s.length <= 5 * 10^4, t.length == s.length,
//              s and t consist of any valid ascii character
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    there is a one to one map f on characters with f(s[i]) = t[i] for every i.
//    f must be a function (same s char -> same t char) and one to one
//    (different s chars -> different t chars).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every pair i < j check: s[i] == s[j] exactly when t[i] == t[j].
//    O(n^2), n = 5 * 10^4 gives about 1.25 * 10^9 pairs. too slow.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    position i only needs to agree with ONE earlier position that has the same
//    character. once s[i] -> t[i] is decided, every later copy of s[i] just has
//    to match it. checking all pairs re-proves the same thing again and again.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    store the map itself. st[a] = the t char that s char a goes to,
//    ts[b] = the s char that goes to t char b. two tables because both
//    directions must be one to one ("foo" -> "bar" breaks st, "ab" -> "aa" breaks ts).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : st(256 , -1), ts(256 , -1). -1 means not decided yet.
//    step 2 : walk i. a = s[i], b = t[i].
//             if both are new, decide st[a] = b and ts[b] = a.
//             else if st[a] != b or ts[b] != a, the map breaks, return false.
//             else the pair agrees with what was decided, move on.
//    step 3 : no pair broke the map, return true.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    step 2 keeps st and ts as inverse maps of each other on the chars seen so far.
//    a pair (a , b) fits iff a and b are both new, or a already goes to b
//    (then b already comes from a). anything else means a needs two images or
//    b needs two sources, which is exactly the statement in 1 failing.
//    time O(n), extra space O(256).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    bool isIsomorphic(string s, string t) {
        ll n = s.size() ;
        // step 1 : st[a] stores where s char a goes, ts[b] stores which s char comes to b
        vector < ll > st(256 , -1) ;
        vector < ll > ts(256 , -1) ;
        // step 2 : check every pair (s[i] , t[i])
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            ll a = (unsigned char)s[i] ;
            ll b = (unsigned char)t[i] ;
            if(st[a] == -1 && ts[b] == -1)
            {
                st[a] = b ;
                ts[b] = a ;
            }
            else if(st[a] != b || ts[b] != a)
            {
                return false ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 3
        return true ;
    }
};
290. Word Pattern Easy

LeetCode · cf/Word_Pattern.cpp

// orz
// 290. Word Pattern · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given a pattern and a string s, return true if s follows the same pattern.
// follow means a bijection between the letters of pattern and the non-empty
// words of s: each letter maps to exactly one word, each word maps to exactly
// one letter, no two letters map to the same word and no two words to the same letter.
// Example 1: pattern = "abba", s = "dog cat cat dog"    -> true
// Example 2: pattern = "abba", s = "dog cat cat fish"   -> false
// Example 3: pattern = "aaaa", s = "dog cat cat dog"    -> false
// Constraints: 1 <= pattern.length <= 300, pattern is lowercase English letters,
//              1 <= s.length <= 3000, s is lowercase English letters and ' ',
//              s has no leading or trailing spaces, words are split by a single space
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    split s into words w0 .. w(k - 1). need k == pattern.length and a one to one
//    map f from letters to words with f(pattern[i]) = wi for every i.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    split s, then for every pair i < j check: pattern[i] == pattern[j] exactly
//    when wi == wj. O(k^2) word compares. fine at k = 300, but it is the same
//    waste as 205.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    once letter c is tied to a word, every later c only has to match that one word.
//    comparing all pairs re-proves the same tie again and again.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    this is 205 Isomorphic Strings with words in place of t's characters.
//    lw[c] = the word letter c goes to, wl[word] = the letter that goes to the word.
//    both directions, because both must be one to one.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : split s on ' ' into words.
//    step 2 : different counts of letters and words can never match, return false.
//    step 3 : walk i. c = pattern[i], w = words[i].
//             if both are new, tie them: lw[c] = w, wl[w] = c.
//             else if lw[c] != w or wl[w] != c, the map breaks, return false.
//             else they agree, move on.
//    step 4 : nothing broke, return true.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    same as 205: lw and wl stay inverse maps of each other, and a pair fails
//    exactly when a letter would need two words or a word two letters.
//    time O(|s| log k) (string compares in the map), extra space O(|s|).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    bool wordPattern(string pattern, string s) {
        ll n = s.size() ;
        // step 1 : split s into words
        vector < string > words ;
        string cur = "" ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            if(s[i] == ' ')
            {
                words.push_back(cur) ;
                cur = "" ;
            }
            else
            {
                cur += s[i] ;
            }
        }
        words.push_back(cur) ;
        // step 2 : the counts must match
        ll k = pattern.size() ;
        if((ll)words.size() != k)
        {
            return false ;
        }
        // step 3 : lw[c] stores the word letter c goes to ("" = not tied yet)
        //          wl stores, for every tied word, the letter that goes to it
        vector < string > lw(26 , "") ;
        map < string , char > wl ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < k ; i++)
        {
            ll c = pattern[i] - 'a' ;
            string w = words[i] ;
            if(lw[c] == "" && wl.find(w) == wl.end())
            {
                lw[c] = w ;
                wl[w] = pattern[i] ;
            }
            else if(lw[c] != w || wl[w] != pattern[i])
            {
                return false ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return true ;
    }
};
242. Valid Anagram Easy

LeetCode · cf/Valid_Anagram.cpp

// orz
// 242. Valid Anagram · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given two strings s and t, return true if t is an anagram of s (the same
// letters, each used the same number of times, in any order), and false otherwise.
// Example 1: s = "anagram", t = "nagaram"   -> true
// Example 2: s = "rat", t = "car"           -> false
// Constraints: 1 <= s.length, t.length <= 5 * 10^4, s and t are lowercase English letters
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    for every letter c: (number of c in s) == (number of c in t).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every letter of s, scan t for an unused equal letter and mark it used.
//    at the end, t must have nothing left over.
//    O(n^2), n = 5 * 10^4 gives 2.5 * 10^9. too slow.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it looks for positions, but positions don't matter in an anagram.
//    only the count of each letter matters.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    same as 383 Ransom Note: a letter is just a count. one counter per letter,
//    +1 for s, -1 for t. anagram iff every counter ends at 0.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : different lengths can never be anagrams, return false.
//    step 2 : cnt[c]++ for every letter of s, cnt[c]-- for every letter of t.
//    step 3 : if some cnt[c] != 0, one string has more c, return false.
//    step 4 : return true.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after step 2, cnt[c] = (c in s) - (c in t). all 26 are 0 exactly when
//    the statement in 1 holds.
//    time O(n), extra space O(26).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    bool isAnagram(string s, string t) {
        ll n = s.size() ;
        ll m = t.size() ;
        // step 1 : lengths must match
        if(n != m)
        {
            return false ;
        }
        // step 2 : cnt[c] stores (c in s) - (c in t)
        vector < ll > cnt(26 , 0) ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            cnt[s[i] - 'a']++ ;
            cnt[t[i] - 'a']-- ;
        }
        // step 3 : every letter must balance
        for(ll c = 0 ; c < 26 ; c++)
        {
            if(cnt[c] != 0)
            {
                return false ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return true ;
    }
};
49. Group Anagrams Medium

LeetCode · cf/Group_Anagrams.cpp

// orz
// 49. Group Anagrams · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an array of strings strs, group the anagrams together (strings with the
// same letters, each used the same number of times). return the groups in any order.
// Example 1: strs = ["eat","tea","tan","ate","nat","bat"]
//            -> [["bat"],["nat","tan"],["ate","eat","tea"]]
// Example 2: strs = [""]    -> [[""]]
// Example 3: strs = ["a"]   -> [["a"]]
// Constraints: 1 <= strs.length <= 10^4, 0 <= strs[i].length <= 100,
//              strs[i] is lowercase English letters
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    a ~ b iff a is an anagram of b. this is an equivalence relation.
//    output its classes.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every string, compare it with the first string of every group so far
//    (242 Valid Anagram, O(L)). join the first group that matches, else open a new one.
//    O(n^2 * L) = 10^8 * 100. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it compares a string against every group to find its class, when the class
//    could be read off the string alone.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    give every class a name that each member can compute alone: the sorted string.
//    "eat", "tea", "ate" all sort to "aet". a ~ b iff sorted(a) == sorted(b).
//    then a map from name to group index finds the group in one lookup.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : for every string w, key = w sorted.
//    step 2 : if key is new, id[key] = groups.size(), open an empty group.
//    step 3 : push w into groups[id[key]].
//    step 4 : return groups.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    two strings are anagrams exactly when they have the same letter counts,
//    and the sorted string is those counts written out in order. so equal keys
//    = same class, and every class gets exactly one group.
//    time O(n * L log L + n * L log n) for the sorts and the map, extra space O(n * L).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    vector<vector<string>> groupAnagrams(vector<string>& strs) {
        ll n = strs.size() ;
        vector < vector < string > > groups ;
        // this stores, for every key, the index of its group in groups
        map < string , ll > id ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            // step 1 : the name of the class
            string key = strs[i] ;
            sort(key.begin() , key.end()) ;
            // step 2 : open a new group for a new key
            if(id.find(key) == id.end())
            {
                id[key] = groups.size() ;
                groups.push_back(vector < string > ()) ;
            }
            // step 3
            groups[id[key]].push_back(strs[i]) ;
        }
        // step 4
        return groups ;
    }
};
1. Two Sum Easy

LeetCode · cf/Two_Sum.cpp

// orz
// 1. Two Sum · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given an array of integers nums and an integer target, return the indices of
// the two numbers that add up to target. there is exactly one solution, and the
// same element may not be used twice. return the answer in any order.
// Example 1: nums = [2,7,11,15], target = 9   -> [0,1]
// Example 2: nums = [3,2,4], target = 6       -> [1,2]
// Example 3: nums = [3,3], target = 6         -> [0,1]
// Constraints: 2 <= n <= 10^4, -10^9 <= nums[i] <= 10^9, -10^9 <= target <= 10^9,
//              only one valid answer exists
// Follow up: an algorithm less than O(n^2) time. -> done here.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    find i != j with nums[i] + nums[j] = target.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    try every pair i < j. O(n^2) = 10^8 / 2. passes, but the follow up asks for less.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    for a fixed i the partner is forced: it must be rem = target - nums[i].
//    the inner loop scans all of j just to answer "where is rem?".
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    "where is value rem?" is a lookup. a map value -> index answers it in O(log n).
//    then the pair search is one loop.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : pos[v] = (last index of v) + 1. +1 so that 0 means "v is not there".
//             keeping the LAST index matters for [3,3]: pos[3] = 2, so i = 0 finds index 1.
//    step 2 : for every i, rem = target - nums[i] (in ll, it can reach 2 * 10^9).
//             if rem is in pos and pos[rem] != i + 1 (not i itself), return {i , pos[rem] - 1}.
//    step 3 : the statement promises an answer, so step 2 always returns.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    take the answer pair i < j. if nums[i] != nums[j], pos[nums[j]] points at
//    some index holding nums[j], which is not i, so i finds a valid partner.
//    if nums[i] == nums[j], pos holds the last copy, which is >= j > i, so again not i.
//    time O(n log n), extra space O(n).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    vector<int> twoSum(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 1 : pos stores, for every value, its last index + 1
        map < ll , ll > pos ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            pos[nums[i]] = i + 1 ;
        }
        // step 2 : the partner of i must be rem
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            ll rem = (ll)target - nums[i] ;
            if(pos.find(rem) != pos.end() && pos[rem] != i + 1)
            {
                return {(int)i , (int)(pos[rem] - 1)} ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 3 : never reached, an answer always exists
        return {} ;
    }
};
202. Happy Number Easy

LeetCode · cf/Happy_Number.cpp

// orz
// 202. Happy Number · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// a happy number: start with n, replace it by the sum of the squares of its
// digits, and repeat. if this reaches 1, n is happy. otherwise it loops forever
// in a cycle that does not contain 1. return true if n is happy.
// Example 1: n = 19 -> true   (1 + 81 = 82, 64 + 4 = 68, 36 + 64 = 100, 1 + 0 + 0 = 1)
// Example 2: n = 2  -> false
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 2^31 - 1
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    f(x) = sum of (digit of x)^2. the sequence x0 = n, x(k + 1) = f(xk).
//    is there a k with xk = 1?
//
// 2. Brute force
//    apply f a fixed big number of times (say 10^6) and see if 1 ever shows up.
//    works, but "big enough" is a guess, not a proof.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the moment a value repeats, the sequence is stuck in a cycle forever.
//    every step after the first repeat is wasted, and the guess hides that.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    the values are small after one step: n < 10^10 has at most 10 digits, so
//    f(n) <= 10 * 81 = 810. so the sequence lives in 1 .. 810 from x1 on, and must
//    repeat within ~812 steps. keep a set of seen values: a repeat = a cycle.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : nxt(x) returns the sum of the squares of the digits of x.
//    step 2 : walk x = n while x != 1. if x is already in seen, it is a cycle
//             without 1 (1 would have stopped the walk), return false.
//             else put x in seen and move x = nxt(x).
//    step 3 : the walk reached 1, return true.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    f is a function, so once some x repeats, everything after it repeats too.
//    the walk either hits 1 or hits a repeat, and by 4 a repeat comes in at most
//    ~812 steps. a repeat before 1 means 1 is never reached.
//    time O(812 * 10), extra space O(812).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
ll nxt(ll x)
{
    // step 1 : this returns the sum of the squares of the digits of x
    ll sum = 0 ;
    while(x > 0)
    {
        ll d = x % 10 ;
        sum += d * d ;
        x /= 10 ;
    }
    return sum ;
}
class Solution {
public:
    bool isHappy(int n) {
        // step 2 : walk until 1 or until a value repeats
        set < ll > seen ;
        ll x = n ;
        while(x != 1)
        {
            if(seen.find(x) != seen.end())
            {
                return false ;
            }
            else
            {
                seen.insert(x) ;
                x = nxt(x) ;
            }
        }
        // step 3
        return true ;
    }
};
219. Contains Duplicate II Easy

LeetCode · cf/Contains_Duplicate_II.cpp

// orz
// 219. Contains Duplicate II · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given an integer array nums and an integer k, return true if there are two
// distinct indices i and j with nums[i] == nums[j] and abs(i - j) <= k.
// Example 1: nums = [1,2,3,1], k = 3       -> true
// Example 2: nums = [1,0,1,1], k = 1       -> true
// Example 3: nums = [1,2,3,1,2,3], k = 2   -> false
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 10^5, -10^9 <= nums[i] <= 10^9, 0 <= k <= 10^5
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    is there i < j with nums[i] == nums[j] and j - i <= k?
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every j, look back at i = j - 1 .. j - k for an equal value.
//    O(n * k) = 10^5 * 10^5 = 10^10. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    for a fixed j, among all earlier i with nums[i] == nums[j], the closest one
//    gives the smallest j - i. if the closest one is too far, all of them are.
//    so only ONE earlier index per value matters: the last one.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    last[v] = the last index where v was seen. then each j is one lookup:
//    is j - last[nums[j]] <= k?
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : walk j = 0 .. n - 1. if nums[j] was seen and j - last[nums[j]] <= k,
//             return true.
//    step 2 : last[nums[j]] = j (j is now the closest copy for everything after it).
//    step 3 : no pair found, return false.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    if a good pair i < j exists, at time j last[nums[j]] is the largest i' < j with
//    the same value, so i' >= i and j - i' <= j - i <= k. step 1 catches it.
//    time O(n log n), extra space O(n).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    bool containsNearbyDuplicate(vector<int>& nums, int k) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // this stores, for every value, the last index where it was seen
        map < ll , ll > last ;
        for(ll j = 0 ; j < n ; j++)
        {
            // step 1 : the closest earlier copy of nums[j]
            if(last.find(nums[j]) != last.end() && j - last[nums[j]] <= k)
            {
                return true ;
            }
            // step 2
            last[nums[j]] = j ;
        }
        // step 3
        return false ;
    }
};
128. Longest Consecutive Sequence Medium

LeetCode · cf/Longest_Consecutive_Sequence.cpp

// orz
// 128. Longest Consecutive Sequence · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an unsorted array of integers nums, return the length of the longest
// run of consecutive values (x, x + 1, .., x + len - 1) that are all in nums.
// you must write an algorithm that runs in O(n) time.
// Example 1: nums = [100,4,200,1,3,2]         -> 4   (1, 2, 3, 4)
// Example 2: nums = [0,3,7,2,5,8,4,6,0,1]     -> 9   (0 .. 8)
// Example 3: nums = [1,0,1,2]                 -> 3
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 10^5, -10^9 <= nums[i] <= 10^9
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    S = the set of values in nums. the largest len with x, x + 1, .., x + len - 1
//    all in S for some x (0 if S is empty).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every x in S, count up x + 1, x + 2, .. while they are in S. keep the best.
//    a run of length L gets walked from each of its L values: L + (L - 1) + .. = O(L^2).
//    nums = 0 .. 10^5 - 1 gives ~5 * 10^9. dead.
//    (sorting + one scan is O(n log n), but the judge asks for O(n).)
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    walking up from x + 1, x + 2, .. re-walks a tail of the walk that started at x.
//    only the walk from the FIRST value of a run is needed.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    x is the first value of its run exactly when x - 1 is not in S.
//    walk up only from those starts. every value of S is then walked exactly once,
//    by the start of its own run. with a hash set each "is v in S" is O(1), so O(n).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : s = unordered_set of nums (duplicates collapse).
//    step 2 : for every x in s (not in nums: duplicate starts would re-walk the run):
//             if x - 1 is in s, x is not a start, skip it.
//    step 3 : else len = 1, and while x + len is in s, len++. ans = max(ans , len).
//    step 4 : return ans (0 when nums is empty, the loop never runs).
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every run has exactly one start, and step 3 from that start counts the whole run.
//    each value is visited by step 2 once and by step 3 once (inside its own run),
//    so the total work is O(n) average with the hash set.
//    time O(n) average, extra space O(n).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int longestConsecutive(vector<int>& nums) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 1 : s stores every value once
        // note : unordered_set is O(1) average, an anti-hash test could slow it down
        unordered_set < ll > s ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            s.insert(nums[i]) ;
        }
        ll ans = 0 ;
        for(auto x : s)
        {
            // step 2 : only walk from the first value of a run
            if(s.find(x - 1) != s.end())
            {
                continue ;
            }
            // step 3 : walk the run up from x
            ll len = 1 ;
            while(s.find(x + len) != s.end())
            {
                len++ ;
            }
            ans = max(ans , len) ;
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};

Intervals

228. Summary Ranges Easy

LeetCode · cf/Summary_Ranges.cpp

// orz
// 228. Summary Ranges · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given a sorted array of unique integers nums, return the smallest sorted list
// of ranges that covers every number of nums exactly (no range holds a number
// that is not in nums). write range [a, b] as "a->b" if a != b and as "a" if a == b.
// Example 1: nums = [0,1,2,4,5,7]     -> ["0->2","4->5","7"]
// Example 2: nums = [0,2,3,4,6,8,9]   -> ["0","2->4","6","8->9"]
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 20, -2^31 <= nums[i] <= 2^31 - 1,
//              all values are unique, nums is sorted ascending
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    cut nums into maximal blocks where each value is the previous + 1.
//    output each block as its first and last value.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every pair (i , j) check if nums[i .. j] is consecutive, and keep only the
//    blocks that can't be grown left or right. O(n^3), tiny at n = 20, but clumsy.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the blocks don't overlap and come in order. once a block ends at h, the next
//    one starts at h + 1, so there is nothing to search for.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    my t / h window: t = start of the block, push h forward while the next value
//    is nums[h] + 1. when h can't move, [t, h] is one maximal block, and the next
//    block starts at t = h + 1.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : t = 0. while t < n:
//    step 2 : h = t. while h + 1 < n and nums[h + 1] == nums[h] + 1, h++.
//             (in ll: nums[h] + 1 overflows int at 2^31 - 1.)
//    step 3 : write the block: "a" if t == h, else "a->b". then t = h + 1.
//    step 4 : return the list.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    step 2 stops exactly at a gap (or the end), so [t, h] can't grow right, and it
//    can't grow left because the block before it stopped at a gap. so every block
//    is maximal and together they cover nums once, in order. maximal blocks are
//    the fewest ranges, since no range can jump over a gap.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1) besides the answer.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    vector<string> summaryRanges(vector<int>& nums) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        vector < string > ans ;
        // step 1 : t is the start of the current block
        ll t = 0 ;
        while(t < n)
        {
            // step 2 : grow h while the next value is one more
            ll h = t ;
            while(h + 1 < n && (ll)nums[h + 1] == (ll)nums[h] + 1)
            {
                h++ ;
            }
            // step 3 : write [t, h]
            if(t == h)
            {
                ans.push_back(to_string(nums[t])) ;
            }
            else
            {
                ans.push_back(to_string(nums[t]) + "->" + to_string(nums[h])) ;
            }
            t = h + 1 ;
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};
56. Merge Intervals Medium

LeetCode · cf/Merge_Intervals.cpp

// orz
// 56. Merge Intervals · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an array of intervals where intervals[i] = [start, end], merge all the
// overlapping intervals (touching ones like [1,4] and [4,5] overlap too), and
// return the non-overlapping intervals that cover exactly the same points.
// Example 1: intervals = [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]]   -> [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]]
// Example 2: intervals = [[1,4],[4,5]]                  -> [[1,5]]
// Example 3: intervals = [[4,7],[1,4]]                  -> [[1,7]]
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 10^4, intervals[i].length == 2, 0 <= start <= end <= 10^4
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    U = union of all [start, end]. output the connected pieces of U, each as
//    [its min, its max], in any order.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    keep merging: find any two intervals that overlap, replace them by their union,
//    repeat until no pair overlaps. each round is O(n^2) pair checks, up to n rounds.
//    O(n^3) = 10^12. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it compares intervals that are far apart and can never touch. if the intervals
//    are in order of start, an interval can only touch the piece that is being
//    built right now, never an older one.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    sort by start. then it is my painted bricks sweep: keep the current piece
//    [curl, curr]. the next interval either starts inside it (l <= curr: extend curr)
//    or starts after it (l > curr: the piece is finished, flush it, start a new one).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : sort the intervals by start (sort on vector < int > compares start first).
//    step 2 : curl, curr = the first interval.
//    step 3 : for every next [l , r]:
//             if l <= curr, it overlaps or touches: curr = max(curr , r).
//             else flush [curl , curr] and start the new piece [l , r].
//    step 4 : flush the last piece after the loop, return.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after the sort, every later interval starts at l >= curl. if l > curr then
//    every later one also starts after curr, so nothing can join the piece anymore:
//    flushing it is safe. if l <= curr the two touch, so they belong to one piece.
//    time O(n log n) for the sort, extra space O(1) besides the answer.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    vector<vector<int>> merge(vector<vector<int>>& intervals) {
        ll n = intervals.size() ;
        // step 1 : sort by start
        sort(intervals.begin() , intervals.end()) ;
        // step 2 : the piece being built
        vector < vector < int > > ans ;
        ll curl = intervals[0][0] ;
        ll curr = intervals[0][1] ;
        // step 3 : extend or flush
        for(ll i = 1 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            ll l = intervals[i][0] ;
            ll r = intervals[i][1] ;
            if(l <= curr)
            {
                curr = max(curr , r) ;
            }
            else
            {
                ans.push_back({(int)curl , (int)curr}) ;
                curl = l ;
                curr = r ;
            }
        }
        // step 4 : the last piece
        ans.push_back({(int)curl , (int)curr}) ;
        return ans ;
    }
};
57. Insert Interval Medium

LeetCode · cf/Insert_Interval.cpp

// orz
// 57. Insert Interval · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an array of non-overlapping intervals sorted by start, and one more
// interval newInterval = [start, end], insert newInterval so that the intervals
// stay sorted by start and non-overlapping (merge where needed, touching counts
// as overlapping). return the result.
// Example 1: intervals = [[1,3],[6,9]], newInterval = [2,5]   -> [[1,5],[6,9]]
// Example 2: intervals = [[1,2],[3,5],[6,7],[8,10],[12,16]], newInterval = [4,8]
//            -> [[1,2],[3,10],[12,16]]
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 10^4, intervals[i].length == 2, 0 <= start <= end <= 10^5,
//              intervals is sorted by start and non-overlapping,
//              newInterval.length == 2, 0 <= start <= end <= 10^5
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    output the connected pieces of (union of intervals) + [L, R], sorted,
//    where [L, R] = newInterval.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    append newInterval and run 56 Merge Intervals: sort + sweep. O(n log n).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the input is already sorted and already merged. only one interval is out of
//    place, and the sort spends n log n to fix one position.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    against [L, R] every old interval [l, r] is one of three kinds, and they come
//    in this order: fully left (r < L), touching (l <= R and r >= L), fully right
//    (l > R). the touching ones all melt into [L, R]. so: copy, melt, copy. one pass.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : i = 0. while intervals[i] ends before L (r < L), copy it.
//    step 2 : while intervals[i] starts at or before R (l <= R), it touches:
//             L = min(L , l), R = max(R , r).
//    step 3 : push the melted [L, R].
//    step 4 : copy every interval that is left (all start after R), return.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    sorted and disjoint means the ends are sorted too, so the r < L ones form a
//    prefix. after them every interval has r >= L, so it touches [L, R] exactly
//    when l <= R, and since the starts are sorted the touching ones come first.
//    R only grows while melting, so the melt loop takes exactly them. the rest
//    start after the final R and stay as they are, already sorted.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1) besides the answer.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    vector<vector<int>> insert(vector<vector<int>>& intervals, vector<int>& newInterval) {
        ll n = intervals.size() ;
        ll L = newInterval[0] ;
        ll R = newInterval[1] ;
        vector < vector < int > > ans ;
        // step 1 : copy the intervals fully left of [L, R]
        ll i = 0 ;
        while(i < n && intervals[i][1] < L)
        {
            ans.push_back(intervals[i]) ;
            i++ ;
        }
        // step 2 : melt every interval that touches [L, R]
        while(i < n && intervals[i][0] <= R)
        {
            L = min(L , (ll)intervals[i][0]) ;
            R = max(R , (ll)intervals[i][1]) ;
            i++ ;
        }
        // step 3
        ans.push_back({(int)L , (int)R}) ;
        // step 4 : copy the intervals fully right
        while(i < n)
        {
            ans.push_back(intervals[i]) ;
            i++ ;
        }
        return ans ;
    }
};
452. Minimum Number of Arrows to Burst Balloons Medium

LeetCode · cf/Minimum_Number_of_Arrows_to_Burst_Balloons.cpp

// orz
// 452. Minimum Number of Arrows to Burst Balloons · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// balloons are on a wall, balloon i spans points[i] = [xstart, xend] on the x axis.
// an arrow shot straight up at x bursts every balloon with xstart <= x <= xend,
// and keeps flying. return the minimum number of arrows that burst all balloons.
// Example 1: points = [[10,16],[2,8],[1,6],[7,12]]   -> 2   (x = 6 and x = 11)
// Example 2: points = [[1,2],[3,4],[5,6],[7,8]]      -> 4
// Example 3: points = [[1,2],[2,3],[3,4],[4,5]]      -> 2   (x = 2 and x = 4)
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 10^5, points[i].length == 2, -2^31 <= xstart < xend <= 2^31 - 1
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    the smallest set of points X such that every interval [a, b] holds some x in X.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    an arrow can always slide right to the nearest balloon end, so only the n ends
//    matter. try every subset of ends, keep the smallest that hits every balloon.
//    O(2^n * n). dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it tries arrows in places that are clearly worse. look at the balloon that ends
//    first: some arrow must hit it, so some arrow is at x <= its end. moving that
//    arrow right to exactly its end only gains balloons (every balloon that the
//    arrow hit still holds the end). so the first arrow can be fixed.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    that is my Movie Festival greedy: sort by end, shoot at the first end, skip every
//    balloon the arrow already hits (start <= arrow), and the first balloon that
//    starts after the arrow forces the next arrow at its own end.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : sort the balloons by end.
//    step 2 : last = -INF (position of the last arrow, below every start).
//    step 3 : for every balloon [a, b] in that order:
//             if a > last, the last arrow misses it: shoot a new arrow at b, ans++, last = b.
//             else the last arrow already bursts it (a <= last <= b), skip.
//    step 4 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    in end order, last is always the end of some earlier balloon, so last <= b for
//    every balloon still to come. so "a <= last" really means the arrow hits it.
//    a balloon with a > last is missed by every arrow so far and ends first among the
//    rest, so by 3 an optimal answer puts its next arrow exactly at b.
//    time O(n log n) for the sort, extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int findMinArrowShots(vector<vector<int>>& points) {
        ll n = points.size() ;
        // step 1 : sort by end
        sort(points.begin() , points.end() , [](const vector < int > &a , const vector < int > &b)
        {
            return a[1] < b[1] ;
        }) ;
        // step 2 : no arrow yet, -INF is below every start
        ll last = -INF ;
        ll ans = 0 ;
        // step 3 : shoot only when the last arrow misses
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            ll a = points[i][0] ;
            ll b = points[i][1] ;
            if(a > last)
            {
                ans++ ;
                last = b ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};

20. Valid Parentheses Easy

LeetCode · cf/Valid_Parentheses.cpp

// orz
// 20. Valid Parentheses · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given a string s of just the characters '(', ')', '{', '}', '[' and ']',
// return true if it is valid: every open bracket is closed by the same type of
// bracket, open brackets are closed in the correct order, and every close
// bracket has a matching open bracket.
// Example 1: s = "()"       -> true
// Example 2: s = "()[]{}"   -> true
// Example 3: s = "(]"       -> false
// Example 4: s = "([])"     -> true
// Example 5: s = "([)]"     -> false
// Constraints: 1 <= s.length <= 10^4, s is only '()[]{}'
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    s is valid iff it reduces to "" by repeatedly deleting an adjacent pair
//    "()", "[]" or "{}".
//
// 2. Brute force
//    do exactly that: scan for an adjacent matching pair, delete it, repeat.
//    up to n / 2 rounds of O(n) each, O(n^2) = 10^8 / 2. slow and clumsy.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    each round rescans the whole string from the start, but the only pair that
//    can appear new after a deletion is right where the deletion happened.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    a close bracket must match the LAST open bracket that is still open.
//    "last open, first closed" is a stack.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : open bracket: push it.
//    step 2 : close bracket: want = its matching open bracket.
//             if the stack is empty (nothing to close) or the top is not want, return false.
//             else pop: this pair is done.
//    step 3 : at the end, valid iff nothing is left open (stack empty).
//
// 6. Why it works
//    the stack holds exactly the brackets that are open and not closed yet, in order.
//    deleting the pair (top, current close) is one deletion of the brute force, done
//    at the only place a new pair can appear. so the scan succeeds iff the
//    deletions reduce s to "".
//    time O(n), extra space O(n).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    bool isValid(string s) {
        ll n = s.size() ;
        // this stores the open brackets that are not closed yet
        stack < char > st ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            char c = s[i] ;
            // step 1 : open bracket
            if(c == '(' || c == '[' || c == '{')
            {
                st.push(c) ;
                continue ;
            }
            // step 2 : close bracket, it must match the last open one
            char want ;
            if(c == ')')
            {
                want = '(' ;
            }
            else if(c == ']')
            {
                want = '[' ;
            }
            else
            {
                want = '{' ;
            }
            if(st.empty() || st.top() != want)
            {
                return false ;
            }
            else
            {
                st.pop() ;
            }
        }
        // step 3 : nothing may stay open
        return st.empty() ;
    }
};
71. Simplify Path Medium

LeetCode · cf/Simplify_Path.cpp

// orz
// 71. Simplify Path · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an absolute Unix-style path (it starts with '/'), return its simplified
// canonical path. the rules of the file system:
//   '.' is the current directory, '..' is the parent directory, and any run of
//   slashes like '//' counts as one '/'. any other name made of periods, like
//   '...' or '....', is a normal file or directory name.
// the canonical path starts with a single '/', directories are split by a single
// '/', it does not end with '/' (unless it is just the root), and it has no '.'
// or '..' parts.
// Example 1: path = "/home/"                            -> "/home"
// Example 2: path = "/home//foo/"                       -> "/home/foo"
// Example 3: path = "/home/user/Documents/../Pictures"  -> "/home/user/Pictures"
// Example 4: path = "/../"                              -> "/"
// Example 5: path = "/.../a/../b/c/../d/./"             -> "/.../b/d"
// Constraints: 1 <= path.length <= 3000, path is English letters, digits, '.', '/'
//              or '_', path is a valid absolute Unix path
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    cut path at every '/' into parts. start at the root and read the parts left to
//    right: "" and "." stay where you are, ".." goes up one level (the root stays the
//    root), any other part goes down into that directory. output where you end up,
//    as '/' + names joined by '/' ("/" for the root).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    rewrite the string until it stops changing: squash "//" to "/", delete "/./",
//    delete "/name/..", fix the ends. each rewrite is O(n), up to O(n) rewrites,
//    O(n^2), and the edge cases ("/..." vs "/..", the root) are easy to get wrong.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it rescans the whole string after every rewrite. but ".." only ever cancels
//    the LAST directory that is still open, so the open directories can be kept
//    in order as they are read.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    the current directory is a list of names from the root. going down pushes a
//    name, ".." pops the last one. "last in, first out" is a stack.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : split path on '/' into parts (runs of '/' make "" parts).
//    step 2 : for every part: "" or "." -> skip.
//             ".." -> pop the last name if there is one (at the root, stay).
//             anything else -> push it.
//    step 3 : join: ans = "/" + name for every name on the stack, bottom to top.
//    step 4 : an empty stack means the root, ans = "/".
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after each part the stack is exactly the list of directories from the root to
//    where the path stands now, so after the last part it is the final directory.
//    step 3 writes it in the canonical form: one '/' before each name, no trailing '/'.
//    time O(n), extra space O(n).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    string simplifyPath(string path) {
        ll n = path.size() ;
        // step 1 : split on '/'
        vector < string > parts ;
        string cur = "" ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            if(path[i] == '/')
            {
                parts.push_back(cur) ;
                cur = "" ;
            }
            else
            {
                cur += path[i] ;
            }
        }
        parts.push_back(cur) ;
        // step 2 : st stores the directories from the root to where we stand
        vector < string > st ;
        for(auto x : parts)
        {
            if(x == "" || x == ".")
            {
                continue ;
            }
            else if(x == "..")
            {
                if(!st.empty())
                {
                    st.pop_back() ;
                }
                continue ;
            }
            else
            {
                st.push_back(x) ;
            }
        }
        // step 3 : join the names
        string ans = "" ;
        for(auto x : st)
        {
            ans += "/" ;
            ans += x ;
        }
        // step 4 : the root
        if(ans == "")
        {
            ans = "/" ;
        }
        return ans ;
    }
};
155. Min Stack Medium

LeetCode · cf/Min_Stack.cpp

// orz
// 155. Min Stack · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// design a stack that supports push, pop, top and getting the minimum element,
// each in O(1) time. implement class MinStack:
//   MinStack()          initializes the stack.
//   void push(int val)  pushes val onto the stack.
//   void pop()          removes the top element.
//   int top()           returns the top element.
//   int getMin()        returns the minimum element in the stack.
// Example 1: ["MinStack","push","push","push","getMin","pop","top","getMin"]
//            [[],[-2],[0],[-3],[],[],[],[]]
//            -> [null,null,null,null,-3,null,0,-2]
// Constraints: -2^31 <= val <= 2^31 - 1, pop, top and getMin are always called
//              on a non-empty stack, at most 3 * 10^4 calls in total
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    keep a sequence v1 .. vk with push / pop at the end, and answer
//    min(v1 .. vk) in O(1).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    a plain stack, and getMin scans every element. O(k) per getMin,
//    up to 3 * 10^4 * 3 * 10^4 = 9 * 10^8, and the judge asks for O(1).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    a stack only changes at the top, so the elements below the top never change.
//    their min is the same every time getMin scans them.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    a prefix min: next to every element store the min of everything from the
//    bottom up to it. pop removes an element and its prefix min together, so the
//    new top's stored min is still right. getMin = the top's stored min.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : st holds pairs (value , min of the stack up to this value).
//    step 2 : push(val): mn = val if the stack is empty, else min(val , st.back().sc).
//             push (val , mn).
//    step 3 : pop(): pop_back.
//    step 4 : top(): st.back().fr.
//    step 5 : getMin(): st.back().sc.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    by induction st[i].sc = min(st[0].fr .. st[i].fr): true for the bottom, and
//    step 2 extends it by one. pop never changes the pairs below. so the top pair
//    always holds the min of the whole stack.
//    time O(1) per call, extra space O(k).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class MinStack {
public:
    // step 1 : st[i] = (value , min of st[0 .. i])
    vector < pll > st ;
    MinStack() {
        st.clear() ;
    }

    void push(int val) {
        // step 2 : the new prefix min
        ll mn = val ;
        if(!st.empty())
        {
            mn = min(mn , st.back().sc) ;
        }
        st.push_back({val , mn}) ;
    }

    void pop() {
        // step 3
        st.pop_back() ;
    }

    int top() {
        // step 4
        return st.back().fr ;
    }

    int getMin() {
        // step 5
        return st.back().sc ;
    }
};
150. Evaluate Reverse Polish Notation Medium

LeetCode · cf/Evaluate_Reverse_Polish_Notation.cpp

// orz
// 150. Evaluate Reverse Polish Notation · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an array of string tokens that is an arithmetic expression in Reverse
// Polish Notation (operator after its two operands), evaluate it and return the
// integer value. operators are '+', '-', '*', '/'. each operand is an integer or
// another expression. division between two integers truncates toward zero.
// there is no division by zero, the input is always a valid RPN expression,
// and the answer and every intermediate result fit in a 32-bit integer.
// Example 1: tokens = ["2","1","+","3","*"]                  -> 9    ((2 + 1) * 3)
// Example 2: tokens = ["4","13","5","/","+"]                 -> 6    (4 + (13 / 5))
// Example 3: tokens = ["10","6","9","3","+","-11","*","/","*","17","+","5","+"] -> 22
// Constraints: 1 <= tokens.length <= 10^4, tokens[i] is an operator or an integer
//              in [-200, 200]
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    RPN: a number is an expression, and "E1 E2 op" is the expression E1 op E2.
//    compute its value.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    find the first operator, apply it to the two numbers right before it, replace
//    the three tokens by the result, and repeat until one token is left.
//    each round is O(n) to find and to shift, n / 2 rounds: O(n^2).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    every round scans again from the start, but the numbers waiting for an
//    operator are always the last ones read, in order.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    "the two numbers right before the operator" = the two most recent unused
//    numbers = the top two of a stack.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : number token: push it. ("-11" is a number, "-" alone is an operator.)
//    step 2 : operator token: b = pop (the right operand, pushed last), a = pop.
//             push a op b. C++ '/' truncates toward zero, which is what is asked.
//    step 3 : at the end the stack holds exactly the value, return it.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after reading a prefix, the stack holds the values of the finished
//    sub-expressions not used yet, left to right. "E1 E2 op" puts E1 then E2 on the
//    stack, so op takes E2 as b and E1 as a, the right order for '-' and '/'.
//    time O(n), extra space O(n).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int evalRPN(vector<string>& tokens) {
        ll n = tokens.size() ;
        // this stores the values of the sub-expressions not used yet
        stack < ll > st ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            string x = tokens[i] ;
            // step 1 : a number
            if(x != "+" && x != "-" && x != "*" && x != "/")
            {
                st.push(stoll(x)) ;
                continue ;
            }
            // step 2 : an operator takes the top two, b is on top
            ll b = st.top() ;
            st.pop() ;
            ll a = st.top() ;
            st.pop() ;
            if(x == "+")
            {
                st.push(a + b) ;
            }
            else if(x == "-")
            {
                st.push(a - b) ;
            }
            else if(x == "*")
            {
                st.push(a * b) ;
            }
            else
            {
                st.push(a / b) ;
            }
        }
        // step 3
        return st.top() ;
    }
};
224. Basic Calculator Hard

LeetCode · cf/Basic_Calculator.cpp

// orz
// 224. Basic Calculator · LeetCode · Hard
//
// Question:
// given a string s that is a valid expression, evaluate it and return the result.
// s has digits, '+', '-', '(', ')' and ' '. you may not use eval().
// '+' is never unary ("+1" and "+(2 + 3)" are invalid). '-' can be unary
// ("-1" and "-(2 + 3)" are valid). there are no two operators in a row.
// every number and every running result fits in a signed 32-bit integer.
// Example 1: s = "1 + 1"                 -> 2
// Example 2: s = " 2-1 + 2 "             -> 3
// Example 3: s = "(1+(4+5+2)-3)+(6+8)"   -> 23
// Constraints: 1 <= s.length <= 3 * 10^5
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    there is no * or /, so the value is a signed sum: every number x in s adds
//    sign(x) * x, where sign(x) = its own sign times the signs in front of every
//    '(' that encloses it.
//    e.g. 1 - (2 - (3 + 4)): 1 -> +1, 2 -> -1, 3 -> (-)(-) = +1, 4 -> +1. value 1 - 2 + 3 + 4 = 6.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    find an innermost "( .. )", evaluate the flat part inside, write the result
//    back into the string, repeat. each round is O(n), up to n / 2 rounds:
//    O(n^2) = (3 * 10^5)^2 = 9 * 10^10. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it rewrites the string over and over. but by 1 each number only needs its
//    final sign, and that sign is known the moment the number is read, from the
//    '(' that are open around it.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    a stack of signs, one per open '(': the top = the product of the signs in
//    front of all the open '('. then one left to right pass adds every number with
//    top * (its own sign).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : ans = 0, sign = +1 (the sign right before the next number or '('),
//             st = [+1] (outside every bracket the multiplier is +1).
//    step 2 : digit: read the whole number num. ans += st.top() * sign * num.
//    step 3 : '+': sign = +1. '-': sign = -1.
//    step 4 : '(': this group's multiplier = st.top() * sign, push it. sign = +1
//             (a fresh start inside the group).
//    step 5 : ')': the group is over, pop its multiplier.
//    step 6 : ' ': skip. return ans after the pass.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    st.top() is always the product of the signs in front of the open '(', so
//    st.top() * sign is exactly sign(x) from 1. a unary '-' at the start or after
//    '(' just sets sign = -1, the same as a binary '-'. so ans is the signed sum in 1.
//    time O(n), extra space O(depth of brackets).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int calculate(string s) {
        ll n = s.size() ;
        // step 1 : st stores the sign multiplier of every open bracket
        ll ans = 0 ;
        ll sign = 1 ;
        stack < ll > st ;
        st.push(1) ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            char c = s[i] ;
            if(c >= '0' && c <= '9')
            {
                // step 2 : read the whole number, add it with its final sign
                ll num = 0 ;
                while(i < n && s[i] >= '0' && s[i] <= '9')
                {
                    num = num * 10 + (s[i] - '0') ;
                    i++ ;
                }
                i-- ; // i is on the last digit, the for loop moves past it
                ans += st.top() * sign * num ;
            }
            // step 3 : the sign of the next term
            else if(c == '+')
            {
                sign = 1 ;
            }
            else if(c == '-')
            {
                sign = -1 ;
            }
            // step 4 : open a group
            else if(c == '(')
            {
                st.push(st.top() * sign) ;
                sign = 1 ;
            }
            // step 5 : close the group
            else if(c == ')')
            {
                st.pop() ;
            }
            // step 6 : a space
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        return ans ;
    }
};

Linked List

141. Linked List Cycle Easy

LeetCode · cf/Linked_List_Cycle.cpp

// orz
// 141. Linked List Cycle · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given head, the head of a linked list, decide whether the list has a cycle in it.
// there is a cycle if some node can be reached again by following next pointers.
// (pos, the index the tail connects to, is only used by the judge to build the list,
// it is not passed in.) return true if there is a cycle, otherwise false.
// Example 1: head = [3,2,0,-4], pos = 1  -> true   (the tail connects to node 1)
// Example 2: head = [1,2], pos = 0       -> true   (the tail connects to node 0)
// Example 3: head = [1], pos = -1        -> false  (no cycle)
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 10^4, -10^5 <= Node.val <= 10^5, pos is -1 or a valid index
// Follow up: can you solve it using O(1) (i.e. constant) memory? -> done here, Floyd.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    walk x0 = head, x(k + 1) = next of xk. either some xk is NULL (the list ends),
//    or the walk never ends, which with n nodes means some node repeats: xi = xj with i < j.
//    answer true exactly in the second case.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    walk the list and put every node pointer in a set. a node already in the set -> cycle.
//    reach NULL -> no cycle. O(n) time but O(n) extra memory, the follow-up bans it.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the set remembers every node ever seen, only to answer one yes / no question:
//    "does the walk come back?". that can be seen without remembering anything.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    two runners on a track: slow moves 1 node per turn, fast moves 2 nodes per turn.
//    if the track ends, fast falls off the end first. if the track has a loop,
//    both get stuck in the loop and fast laps slow: they land on the same node.
//    this is Floyd's tortoise and hare. it needs two pointers only.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : slow = head, fast = head.
//    step 2 : while fast and fast->next are not NULL (so fast can make its 2 steps):
//             slow = slow->next, fast = fast->next->next.
//    step 3 : after the move, if slow == fast, they met inside a cycle -> return true.
//    step 4 : the loop ended, so fast reached NULL, the list ends -> return false.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    no cycle: fast only moves forward, so it hits NULL within n / 2 turns, and slow
//    (always behind fast) can never be on the same node. -> false, correct.
//    cycle of length c, entered after t nodes:
//      - after t turns slow is inside the cycle, and fast (2t steps) is inside too.
//      - let d = how many steps fast is behind slow along the cycle, 0 <= d < c.
//      - each turn slow moves 1 and fast moves 2, so the gap d shrinks by exactly 1.
//        it cannot jump over 0, because it only goes down 1 at a time.
//      - after d more turns d = 0: same node -> true.
//    so they meet within t + c <= n turns.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1): two pointers.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(NULL) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    bool hasCycle(ListNode *head) {
        // step 1 : both runners start at head
        ListNode* slow = head ;
        ListNode* fast = head ;
        // step 2 : slow moves 1, fast moves 2, while fast can still make 2 steps
        while(fast != NULL && fast->next != NULL)
        {
            slow = slow->next ;
            fast = fast->next->next ;
            // step 3 : fast caught up with slow, so they are going round a cycle
            if(slow == fast)
            {
                return true ;
            }
        }
        // step 4 : fast fell off the end, the list ends
        return false ;
    }
};
2. Add Two Numbers Medium

LeetCode · cf/Add_Two_Numbers.cpp

// orz
// 2. Add Two Numbers · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given two non-empty linked lists representing two non-negative integers. the digits
// are stored in reverse order (the ones digit first) and each node holds one digit.
// add the two numbers and return the sum as a linked list in the same reverse order.
// the two numbers have no leading zeros, except the number 0 itself.
// Example 1: l1 = [2,4,3], l2 = [5,6,4]               -> [7,0,8]   (342 + 465 = 807)
// Example 2: l1 = [0], l2 = [0]                       -> [0]
// Example 3: l1 = [9,9,9,9,9,9,9], l2 = [9,9,9,9]     -> [8,9,9,9,0,0,0,1]
// Constraints: 1 <= nodes in each list <= 100, 0 <= Node.val <= 9
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    A = a0 + a1 * 10 + a2 * 10^2 + .., B = b0 + b1 * 10 + ..
//    output the digits s0, s1, .. of A + B, ones digit first.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    turn both lists into integers, add them, turn the sum back into a list.
//    100 digits means numbers up to 10^100, long long holds about 9 * 10^18. dead (overflow).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it builds the whole number when every digit of the sum only needs the two digits
//    in the same column and the carry from the column before.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    this is school column addition, and the lists already start at the ones column,
//    which is exactly where column addition starts. walk both lists together, carry one number.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dummy node, tail = dummy (last node of the answer so far), carry = 0.
//    step 2 : keep going while l1 has digits, or l2 has digits, or carry is not 0.
//    step 3 : s = carry + (digit of l1, 0 if l1 ended) + (digit of l2, 0 if l2 ended),
//             and move l1 / l2 one node forward when they have one.
//    step 4 : the new digit is s % 10, hang it after tail, carry = s / 10.
//    step 5 : return dummy->next.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    column i of A + B is (ai + bi + carry_i) % 10 and sends (ai + bi + carry_i) / 10 to
//    column i + 1. step 3 and 4 do exactly that, column by column. s <= 9 + 9 + 1 = 19,
//    so carry is 0 or 1. a list that ended counts as 0 digits, and the "carry != 0"
//    in step 2 writes the last 1 (999 + 1 = 1000).
//    time O(max(m , n)), extra space O(1) besides the answer list.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* addTwoNumbers(ListNode* l1, ListNode* l2) {
        // step 1 : dummy node, tail = last node of the answer
        ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0) ;
        ListNode* tail = dummy ;
        ll carry = 0 ;
        // step 2 : while some digit or a carry is left
        while(l1 != NULL || l2 != NULL || carry != 0)
        {
            // step 3 : this column's sum
            ll s = carry ;
            if(l1 != NULL)
            {
                s += l1->val ;
                l1 = l1->next ;
            }
            if(l2 != NULL)
            {
                s += l2->val ;
                l2 = l2->next ;
            }
            // step 4 : write the digit, send the carry on
            ListNode* node = new ListNode(s % 10) ;
            tail->next = node ;
            tail = node ;
            carry = s / 10 ;
        }
        // step 5
        return dummy->next ;
    }
};
21. Merge Two Sorted Lists Easy

LeetCode · cf/Merge_Two_Sorted_Lists.cpp

// orz
// 21. Merge Two Sorted Lists · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given the heads of two sorted (non-decreasing) linked lists list1 and list2,
// merge them into one sorted list made by splicing together the nodes of the
// two lists, and return the head of the merged list.
// Example 1: list1 = [1,2,4], list2 = [1,3,4] -> [1,1,2,3,4,4]
// Example 2: list1 = [], list2 = []           -> []
// Example 3: list1 = [], list2 = [0]          -> [0]
// Constraints: 0 <= nodes in each list <= 50, -100 <= Node.val <= 100,
//              both lists are sorted in non-decreasing order
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    a1 <= a2 <= .. <= am and b1 <= b2 <= .. <= bn.
//    chain all m + n nodes into one list c1 <= c2 <= .. <= c(m + n), by changing next pointers only.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    m + n times: scan every node not used yet, take the smallest, hang it at the end.
//    O((m + n)^2).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it scans everything to find the smallest node left, but both lists are sorted,
//    so the smallest node left is always one of the two heads.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    compare only the two heads, take the smaller one: the merge step of merge sort.
//    a dummy node in front means the first taken node needs no special case.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dummy node, tail = dummy (last node of the merged list so far).
//    step 2 : while both lists are non-empty, take the smaller head (list1 on a tie),
//             hang it after tail, move that list one node forward.
//    step 3 : one list is empty now. the other one is sorted and every node in it is
//             >= everything taken, so hang the whole rest after tail at once.
//    step 4 : return dummy->next.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    each step of step 2 takes the smallest node still left (it is one of the two heads),
//    so the merged list grows in sorted order. step 3 adds an already sorted tail whose
//    nodes are all >= the last taken node.
//    time O(m + n), extra space O(1): only the dummy and a few pointers.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* mergeTwoLists(ListNode* list1, ListNode* list2) {
        // step 1 : dummy node, tail = last node of the merged list
        ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0) ;
        ListNode* tail = dummy ;
        // step 2 : take the smaller of the two heads
        while(list1 != NULL && list2 != NULL)
        {
            if(list1->val <= list2->val)
            {
                tail->next = list1 ;
                tail = list1 ;
                list1 = list1->next ;
            }
            else
            {
                tail->next = list2 ;
                tail = list2 ;
                list2 = list2->next ;
            }
        }
        // step 3 : hang the leftover list whole
        if(list1 != NULL)
        {
            tail->next = list1 ;
        }
        else
        {
            tail->next = list2 ;
        }
        // step 4
        return dummy->next ;
    }
};
138. Copy List with Random Pointer Medium

LeetCode · cf/Copy_List_with_Random_Pointer.cpp

// orz
// 138. Copy List with Random Pointer · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// a linked list of length n is given where each node has an extra pointer random,
// which points to any node in the list or to null. make a deep copy: n brand new
// nodes, each with the value of its original, where the next and random pointers of
// the new nodes point to new nodes, so that the copy shows the same list. no pointer
// of the copy may point into the original list. return the head of the copy.
// (the judge shows each node as [val, random_index], random_index = null for no random.)
// Example 1: head = [[7,null],[13,0],[11,4],[10,2],[1,0]] -> [[7,null],[13,0],[11,4],[10,2],[1,0]]
// Example 2: head = [[1,1],[2,1]]                         -> [[1,1],[2,1]]
// Example 3: head = [[3,null],[3,0],[3,null]]             -> [[3,null],[3,0],[3,null]]
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 1000, -10^4 <= Node.val <= 10^4,
//              Node.random is null or points to some node in the linked list
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    original nodes x1 .. xn with next(xi) = x(i + 1) and random(xi) = xj or NULL.
//    build new nodes y1 .. yn with val(yi) = val(xi), next(yi) = y(i + 1),
//    random(yi) = yj exactly when random(xi) = xj (NULL when it is NULL).
//    so the whole job is the map copy(xj) = yj.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    copy the next chain first. then for each xi with random(xi) = xj: walk from head to
//    find j, then walk j steps in the copy to find yj. O(n) per random, O(n^2) total.
//    (a hash map from xj to yj makes it O(n) time but costs O(n) extra memory.)
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    every random pointer asks the same question, "where is the copy of xj?",
//    and answers it by walking from head again (or by keeping a whole map).
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    store the answer in the list itself: put the copy of every node right after it,
//    x1 -> y1 -> x2 -> y2 -> .. -> xn -> yn. now copy(x) = x->next, an O(1) lookup with no map.
//    afterwards pull the two lists apart again.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : weave. for every original x: make y with the same val,
//             y->next = x->next, x->next = y. then jump to y->next (the next original).
//    step 2 : randoms. for every original x (every other node), with y = x->next:
//             if x->random is NULL, y->random = NULL,
//             else y->random = x->random->next (the copy sits right after its original).
//    step 3 : unweave. dummy node for the copy, tail = dummy. for every original x with
//             y = x->next: x->next = y->next (x gets its old next back),
//             tail->next = y, tail = y.
//             step 3 must be a separate pass after step 2: a later random can point back
//             to an earlier node, and step 2 needs that node still followed by its copy.
//    step 4 : return dummy->next.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after step 1 every original x is followed by its own copy, so x->next = copy(x).
//    step 2 reads only that, so every random of the copy points to the right copy node.
//    step 3 gives every x back the next it had (y->next was the old x->next), so the
//    original list is exactly as before, and chains y1 -> y2 -> .. -> yn in order; the
//    last yn->next is the old xn->next = NULL.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1) besides the n copied nodes themselves.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/*
// Definition for a Node.
class Node {
public:
    int val;
    Node* next;
    Node* random;

    Node(int _val) {
        val = _val;
        next = NULL;
        random = NULL;
    }
};
*/
class Solution {
public:
    Node* copyRandomList(Node* head) {
        // step 1 : weave, every copy sits right after its original
        Node* cur = head ;
        while(cur != NULL)
        {
            Node* copy = new Node(cur->val) ;
            copy->next = cur->next ;
            cur->next = copy ;
            cur = copy->next ;
        }
        // step 2 : random of the copy = the node right after the original's random
        cur = head ;
        while(cur != NULL)
        {
            Node* copy = cur->next ;
            if(cur->random == NULL)
            {
                copy->random = NULL ;
            }
            else
            {
                copy->random = cur->random->next ;
            }
            cur = copy->next ;
        }
        // step 3 : unweave, give the originals their next back, chain the copies
        Node* dummy = new Node(0) ;
        Node* tail = dummy ;
        cur = head ;
        while(cur != NULL)
        {
            Node* copy = cur->next ;
            cur->next = copy->next ;
            tail->next = copy ;
            tail = copy ;
            cur = cur->next ;
        }
        // step 4
        return dummy->next ;
    }
};
92. Reverse Linked List II Medium

LeetCode · cf/Reverse_Linked_List_II.cpp

// orz
// 92. Reverse Linked List II · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given the head of a singly linked list and two integers left <= right,
// reverse the nodes of the list from position left to position right
// (positions start at 1), and return the reversed list.
// Example 1: head = [1,2,3,4,5], left = 2, right = 4 -> [1,4,3,2,5]
// Example 2: head = [5], left = 1, right = 1         -> [5]
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 500, -500 <= Node.val <= 500, 1 <= left <= right <= n
// Follow up: could you do it in one pass? -> done here, one pass.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    list a1 .. an. output a1 .. a(left - 1), a(right), a(right - 1) .. a(left), a(right + 1) .. an,
//    by changing next pointers only, with O(1) extra space.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for k = 0, 1, .. swap the values at positions left + k and right - k, walking
//    from head to find each of them. O(n) per walk, O(n^2) total, and it moves
//    values instead of nodes.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    every walk starts again from head, and it moves the values when only the
//    arrows between the nodes need to turn around.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    reversing a block = reversing the arrows inside it. the block touches the rest
//    of the list through only two arrows: (node before the block -> first node of the block)
//    and (last node of the block -> node after the block). reverse the inside arrows with
//    the three-pointer reverse, then fix those two arrows.
//    a dummy node in front of head makes "the node before the block" exist even when left = 1.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dummy -> head, so position 0 exists and the head needs no special case.
//    step 2 : walk before from dummy, left - 1 steps. now before is at position left - 1.
//    step 3 : first = before->next. after reversing, first becomes the LAST node of the block.
//    step 4 : reverse right - left + 1 nodes: nxt = cur->next, cur->next = prev, prev = cur, cur = nxt.
//             after the loop prev = the old node at right (the new block head),
//             cur = the node at right + 1 (NULL if right = n).
//    step 5 : stitch the two outside arrows: before->next = prev, first->next = cur.
//    step 6 : return dummy->next, because the head itself changes when left = 1.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    nodes outside [left, right] keep their arrows. inside, every arrow is turned around
