//    exactly once by step 4. step 5 fixes the only two arrows that cross the block border.
//    time O(n) in one pass, extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* reverseBetween(ListNode* head, int left, int right) {
        // step 1 : dummy node in front of head
        ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0) ;
        dummy->next = head ;
        // step 2 : before = the node at position left - 1
        ListNode* before = dummy ;
        for(ll i = 1 ; i < left ; i++)
        {
            before = before->next ;
        }
        // step 3 : first node of the block, it ends up last
        ListNode* first = before->next ;
        // step 4 : three-pointer reverse of right - left + 1 nodes
        ListNode* prev = NULL ;
        ListNode* cur = first ;
        for(ll i = left ; i <= right ; i++)
        {
            ListNode* nxt = cur->next ;
            cur->next = prev ;
            prev = cur ;
            cur = nxt ;
        }
        // now prev = new head of the block, cur = node right after the block
        // step 5 : stitch the block back in
        before->next = prev ;
        first->next = cur ;
        // step 6
        return dummy->next ;
    }
};
25. Reverse Nodes in k-Group Hard

LeetCode · cf/Reverse_Nodes_in_k_Group.cpp

// orz
// 25. Reverse Nodes in k-Group · LeetCode · Hard
//
// Question:
// given the head of a linked list, reverse the nodes of the list k at a time and
// return the modified list. k is a positive integer <= the length of the list.
// if the number of nodes is not a multiple of k, the nodes left at the end stay as they are.
// you may not change the values in the nodes, only the nodes themselves.
// Example 1: head = [1,2,3,4,5], k = 2 -> [2,1,4,3,5]
// Example 2: head = [1,2,3,4,5], k = 3 -> [3,2,1,4,5]
// Constraints: 1 <= k <= n <= 5000, 0 <= Node.val <= 1000
// Follow up: can you solve it in O(1) extra memory space? -> done here.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    cut a1 .. an into blocks of k: [a1 .. ak], [a(k + 1) .. a2k], .., and a last part of
//    n % k nodes. reverse every full block in place, keep the last part, keep the block order.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    copy the values into an array, reverse each full block of the array, write the values
//    back. O(n) time but O(n) extra memory, and it changes values, both banned.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it moves the values out and back when only the arrows inside each block need to turn around.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    each full block is Reverse Linked List II on that block: the block touches the rest
//    through two arrows only, (node before -> first) and (last -> node after).
//    turn the inside arrows with the three-pointer reverse, fix those two arrows.
//    after that the old first node is the block's last node, so it is the
//    "node before" of the next block. a dummy node gives the first block a node before.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dummy -> head, before = dummy (the node before the current block).
//    step 2 : count up to k nodes after before. fewer than k -> they stay as they are, stop.
//    step 3 : first = before->next. after reversing, first becomes the LAST node of the block.
//    step 4 : reverse k nodes: nxt = cur->next, cur->next = prev, prev = cur, cur = nxt.
//             after the loop prev = the new block head, cur = the node right after the block.
//    step 5 : stitch the two outside arrows: before->next = prev, first->next = cur.
//    step 6 : before = first, go back to step 2 for the next block.
//    step 7 : return dummy->next, because the head changes (k >= 2) or stays (k = 1).
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every full block gets its inside arrows turned exactly once (step 4) and its two border
//    arrows fixed (step 5), the same argument as Reverse Linked List II. step 6 moves before
//    to the true node before the next block. the last part is never touched because step 2
//    stops before it.
//    time O(n): each node is counted once in step 2 and reversed once in step 4.
//    extra space O(1): a few pointers.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* reverseKGroup(ListNode* head, int k) {
        // step 1 : dummy node in front of head
        ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0) ;
        dummy->next = head ;
        ListNode* before = dummy ;
        while(true)
        {
            // step 2 : are there k nodes after before?
            ListNode* walk = before ;
            ll cnt = 0 ;
            while(cnt < k && walk->next != NULL)
            {
                walk = walk->next ;
                cnt++ ;
            }
            if(cnt < k)
            {
                break ;
            }
            // step 3 : first node of the block, it ends up last
            ListNode* first = before->next ;
            // step 4 : three-pointer reverse of k nodes
            ListNode* prev = NULL ;
            ListNode* cur = first ;
            for(ll i = 0 ; i < k ; i++)
            {
                ListNode* nxt = cur->next ;
                cur->next = prev ;
                prev = cur ;
                cur = nxt ;
            }
            // now prev = new head of the block, cur = node right after the block
            // step 5 : stitch the block back in
            before->next = prev ;
            first->next = cur ;
            // step 6 : the old first is the node before the next block
            before = first ;
        }
        // step 7
        return dummy->next ;
    }
};
19. Remove Nth Node From End of List Medium

LeetCode · cf/Remove_Nth_Node_From_End_of_List.cpp

// orz
// 19. Remove Nth Node From End of List · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given the head of a linked list, remove the n-th node from the end of the list
// and return its head.
// Example 1: head = [1,2,3,4,5], n = 2 -> [1,2,3,5]
// Example 2: head = [1], n = 1         -> []
// Example 3: head = [1,2], n = 1       -> [1]
// Constraints: 1 <= sz <= 30 (sz = number of nodes), 0 <= Node.val <= 100, 1 <= n <= sz
// Follow up: could you do this in one pass? -> done here, one pass.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    list a1 .. aL. the n-th node from the end is a(L - n + 1).
//    make a(L - n) point to a(L - n + 2), where a0 is a dummy so that L - n = 0 also works.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    pass 1: walk the list to count L. pass 2: walk L - n steps from the dummy to
//    reach a(L - n), and skip its next. O(L), but two passes.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    pass 2 walks again over the first L - n nodes that pass 1 already walked over.
//    it only needs to know when L - n steps are done, i.e. when n steps are left.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    a ruler of fixed length n: keep a back pointer exactly n nodes behind a front pointer.
//    when the front pointer stands on the last node aL, the back pointer stands on a(L - n),
//    which is the node pass 2 was looking for.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dummy -> head, so position 0 exists and removing the head needs no special case.
//    step 2 : front = dummy, move front n steps. now front is at position n.
//    step 3 : back = dummy (position 0). the gap is n.
//    step 4 : while front->next is not NULL, move both one step. the gap stays n.
//             at the end front is at position L, so back is at position L - n.
//    step 5 : back->next = back->next->next (skip the n-th node from the end).
//    step 6 : return dummy->next, because the head itself goes when n = L.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    front - back = n all the time. front stops at position L, so back stops at L - n,
//    the node right before a(L - n + 1). n <= L, so step 2 never walks off the list,
//    and back->next exists in step 5.
//    time O(L) in one pass, extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* removeNthFromEnd(ListNode* head, int n) {
        // step 1 : dummy node in front of head
        ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0) ;
        dummy->next = head ;
        // step 2 : front goes n steps ahead
        ListNode* front = dummy ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            front = front->next ;
        }
        // step 3 : back starts at the dummy, n behind front
        ListNode* back = dummy ;
        // step 4 : slide the ruler until front is the last node
        while(front->next != NULL)
        {
            front = front->next ;
            back = back->next ;
        }
        // step 5 : back is right before the node to remove
        back->next = back->next->next ;
        // step 6
        return dummy->next ;
    }
};
82. Remove Duplicates from Sorted List II Medium

LeetCode · cf/Remove_Duplicates_from_Sorted_List_II.cpp

// orz
// 82. Remove Duplicates from Sorted List II · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given the head of a sorted linked list, delete all nodes that have duplicate numbers,
// leaving only the numbers that appear exactly once in the original list.
// return the linked list, still sorted.
// Example 1: head = [1,2,3,3,4,4,5] -> [1,2,5]
// Example 2: head = [1,1,1,2,3]     -> [2,3]
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 300, -100 <= Node.val <= 100, the list is sorted in ascending order
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    cnt(v) = how many nodes hold value v. keep a node exactly when cnt(its value) = 1,
//    in the original order.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every node, walk the whole list to count its value, keep it if the count is 1.
//    O(n^2). (a map of counts makes it O(n) but costs O(n) extra memory.)
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the list is sorted, so all the copies of a value sit next to each other in one run.
//    cnt(v) is just the length of v's run, no need to walk the whole list for it.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    walk the list run by run. a run of length 1 is kept, a longer run is dropped whole.
//    the head itself may be dropped, so build the answer behind a dummy node.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dummy node, tail = dummy (last kept node), cur = head (start of the current run).
//    step 2 : end = cur, move end forward while end->next has the same value as cur.
//             now the run is cur .. end.
//    step 3 : if end == cur, the run has length 1: tail->next = cur, tail = cur.
//             else the value repeats, skip the whole run.
//    step 4 : cur = end->next, the start of the next run. repeat from step 2 while cur != NULL.
//    step 5 : tail->next = NULL, because the last kept node may still point into a dropped run.
//    step 6 : return dummy->next.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    sorted means equal values form one run, so "run length 1" is exactly "cnt(v) = 1".
//    kept nodes are appended in the order they are met, so the answer stays sorted.
//    step 5 cuts off anything after the last kept node.
//    time O(n): every node is passed once by end. extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* deleteDuplicates(ListNode* head) {
        // step 1 : dummy node, tail = last kept node
        ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0) ;
        ListNode* tail = dummy ;
        ListNode* cur = head ;
        while(cur != NULL)
        {
            // step 2 : the run of cur's value is cur .. end
            ListNode* end = cur ;
            while(end->next != NULL && end->next->val == cur->val)
            {
                end = end->next ;
            }
            // step 3 : keep a run of length 1, a longer run is skipped whole
            if(end == cur)
            {
                tail->next = cur ;
                tail = cur ;
            }
            // step 4 : start of the next run
            cur = end->next ;
        }
        // step 5 : cut off whatever was after the last kept node
        tail->next = NULL ;
        // step 6
        return dummy->next ;
    }
};
61. Rotate List Medium

LeetCode · cf/Rotate_List.cpp

// orz
// 61. Rotate List · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given the head of a linked list, rotate the list to the right by k places.
// (one rotation moves the last node to the front.)
// Example 1: head = [1,2,3,4,5], k = 2 -> [4,5,1,2,3]
// Example 2: head = [0,1,2], k = 4     -> [2,0,1]
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 500, -100 <= Node.val <= 100, 0 <= k <= 2 * 10^9
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    list a1 .. an. output a(n - r + 1) .. an, a1 .. a(n - r), where r = k % n.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    k times: walk to the last node and move it to the front. O(n) per rotation,
//    O(n * k) total, k = 2 * 10^9 and n = 500 gives 10^12. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    n rotations give the same list back, so all but k % n rotations are useless.
//    and r single rotations do the same as cutting the list once.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    rotating right by r = cut after the first n - r nodes and swap the two pieces:
//    the old last node points to the old head, node n - r becomes the new last node.
//    only three arrows change.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dummy -> head, so position 0 exists.
//    step 2 : last = dummy, walk to the last node, counting n.
//    step 3 : n = 0 -> return head (nothing to rotate). r = k % n. r = 0 -> return head.
//    step 4 : newtail = dummy, walk n - r steps. now newtail is at position n - r.
//    step 5 : newhead = newtail->next (position n - r + 1).
//             newtail->next = NULL, last->next = head (old head behind the old last).
//    step 6 : return newhead.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after step 5 the list is newhead = a(n - r + 1) .. an = last -> a1 .. a(n - r) = newtail -> NULL,
//    exactly the statement. 1 <= r <= n - 1 here, so newtail is not last and newhead is not NULL.
//    time O(n) (two walks), extra space O(1). k is never looped over, only k % n.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* rotateRight(ListNode* head, int k) {
        // step 1 : dummy node in front of head
        ListNode* dummy = new ListNode(0) ;
        dummy->next = head ;
        // step 2 : find the last node and count n
        ListNode* last = dummy ;
        ll n = 0 ;
        while(last->next != NULL)
        {
            last = last->next ;
            n++ ;
        }
        // step 3 : only k % n rotations matter
        if(n == 0)
        {
            return head ;
        }
        ll r = k % n ;
        if(r == 0)
        {
            return head ;
        }
        // step 4 : newtail = the node at position n - r
        ListNode* newtail = dummy ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n - r ; i++)
        {
            newtail = newtail->next ;
        }
        // step 5 : cut after newtail, hang the old head behind the old last
        ListNode* newhead = newtail->next ;
        newtail->next = NULL ;
        last->next = head ;
        // step 6
        return newhead ;
    }
};
86. Partition List Medium

LeetCode · cf/Partition_List.cpp

// orz
// 86. Partition List · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given the head of a linked list and a value x, partition it so that all nodes less
// than x come before the nodes greater than or equal to x. keep the original relative
// order of the nodes inside each of the two parts.
// Example 1: head = [1,4,3,2,5,2], x = 3 -> [1,2,2,4,3,5]
// Example 2: head = [2,1], x = 2         -> [1,2]
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 200, -100 <= Node.val <= 100, -200 <= x <= 200
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    S = the nodes with val < x in list order, B = the nodes with val >= x in list order.
//    output S followed by B (a stable partition), by changing next pointers only.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    walk once and copy the values < x into new nodes, walk again and copy the values >= x,
//    return the new list. O(n) time but O(n) new nodes, when every node already exists.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it builds a second list. the nodes are already there, only their arrows have to change.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    build the two parts S and B at the same time as two separate chains, each behind
//    its own dummy node. every node goes to the end of its chain, in the order it is met,
//    so each chain keeps the original order. then hang B behind S.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : two dummy nodes small and big, tails stail = small, btail = big.
//    step 2 : walk cur over the list. val < x -> hang cur after stail, else after btail.
//             then cur = cur->next (cur's own next is not touched by hanging it).
//    step 3 : btail->next = NULL, because the last big node may still point at a small node.
//    step 4 : stail->next = big->next (glue B behind S).
//    step 5 : return small->next.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every node lands in exactly one chain, at its end, in the order of the walk, so S and B
//    each keep their relative order. step 3 ends B, step 4 joins S and B.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1): two dummies and a few pointers.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for singly-linked list.
 * struct ListNode {
 *     int val;
 *     ListNode *next;
 *     ListNode() : val(0), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x) : val(x), next(nullptr) {}
 *     ListNode(int x, ListNode *next) : val(x), next(next) {}
 * };
 */
class Solution {
public:
    ListNode* partition(ListNode* head, int x) {
        // step 1 : one dummy per part, tails = last node of each part
        ListNode* small = new ListNode(0) ;
        ListNode* big = new ListNode(0) ;
        ListNode* stail = small ;
        ListNode* btail = big ;
        // step 2 : every node goes to the end of its part
        ListNode* cur = head ;
        while(cur != NULL)
        {
            if(cur->val < x)
            {
                stail->next = cur ;
                stail = cur ;
            }
            else
            {
                btail->next = cur ;
                btail = cur ;
            }
            cur = cur->next ;
        }
        // step 3 : end the big part
        btail->next = NULL ;
        // step 4 : big part behind the small part
        stail->next = big->next ;
        // step 5
        return small->next ;
    }
};
146. LRU Cache Medium

LeetCode · cf/LRU_Cache.cpp

// orz
// 146. LRU Cache · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// design a data structure that follows the Least Recently Used (LRU) cache rules.
// LRUCache(int capacity) makes the cache with a positive size capacity.
// int get(int key) returns the value of key if it is in the cache, otherwise -1.
// void put(int key, int value) updates the value of key if it is there, otherwise
// adds the pair. if that makes the number of keys exceed capacity, evict the least
// recently used key. get and put must each run in O(1) average time.
// (both get and put of a key count as using it.)
// Example 1:
//   ["LRUCache","put","put","get","put","get","put","get","get","get"]
//   [[2],[1,1],[2,2],[1],[3,3],[2],[4,4],[1],[3],[4]]
//   -> [null,null,null,1,null,-1,null,-1,3,4]
//   (put 3 evicts key 2, put 4 evicts key 1)
// Constraints: 1 <= capacity <= 3000, 0 <= key <= 10^4, 0 <= value <= 10^5,
//              at most 2 * 10^5 calls to get and put
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    keep a set of at most capacity pairs (key, value), each with the time it was last used.
//    get / put of a key sets its time to now. when a new key would make the size
//    capacity + 1, remove the pair with the smallest time first.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    a vector of (key, value, time). get / put scan it to find the key, eviction scans it
//    for the smallest time. O(capacity) per call, 2 * 10^5 * 3000 = 6 * 10^8. too slow.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    two scans: one to find a key, one to find the oldest pair. the exact times are never
//    needed, only the ORDER of use, and every use moves one key to the "newest" end.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    keep the pairs in a list ordered by use: newest at the front, oldest at the back.
//    a use = cut the node out and put it at the front. eviction = remove the back node.
//    a doubly linked list does all of that in O(1) once we hold a pointer to the node,
//    and an unordered_map key -> node gives that pointer in O(1).
//    dummy head and dummy tail mean every real node always has a prev and a next,
//    so cutting and inserting never check for NULL.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : head <-> .. <-> tail, two dummy nodes. head->next is the newest pair,
//             tail->prev the oldest. pos[key] = the node holding key.
//    step 2 : unlink(node): node->prev->next = node->next, node->next->prev = node->prev.
//    step 3 : pushfront(head, node): put node between head and head->next.
//    step 4 : get(key): key not in pos -> -1. else unlink + pushfront (it is now the newest),
//             return its value.
//    step 5 : put(key, value), key already in pos: change the value, unlink + pushfront.
//    step 6 : put(key, value), new key: if the cache is full, the oldest node is tail->prev:
//             unlink it, erase its key from pos, delete it. then make the new node,
//             pushfront it, pos[key] = node.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every use moves its node to the front, so the list is always sorted by last use and
//    tail->prev is the least recently used key. pos always holds exactly the keys in the list.
//    every call does one map lookup and a constant number of pointer changes.
//    time O(1) average per call, space O(capacity).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
struct CacheNode
{
    ll key ;
    ll val ;
    CacheNode* prev ;
    CacheNode* next ;
    CacheNode(ll k , ll v)
    {
        key = k ;
        val = v ;
        prev = NULL ;
        next = NULL ;
    }
};
void unlink(CacheNode* node)
{
    // this cuts node out of the list, its neighbours now point to each other
    node->prev->next = node->next ;
    node->next->prev = node->prev ;
}
void pushfront(CacheNode* head , CacheNode* node)
{
    // this puts node right after the dummy head, so it becomes the newest
    node->prev = head ;
    node->next = head->next ;
    head->next->prev = node ;
    head->next = node ;
}
class LRUCache {
public:
    ll cap ;                                  // this stores the capacity
    CacheNode* head ;                         // this stores the dummy in front of the newest
    CacheNode* tail ;                         // this stores the dummy behind the oldest
    unordered_map < ll , CacheNode* > pos ;   // this stores key -> the node holding it

    LRUCache(int capacity) {
        // step 1 : empty list, head <-> tail
        cap = capacity ;
        head = new CacheNode(-1 , -1) ;
        tail = new CacheNode(-1 , -1) ;
        head->next = tail ;
        tail->prev = head ;
        pos.clear() ;
    }

    int get(int key) {
        // step 4 : missing -> -1, else it becomes the newest
        if(pos.find(key) == pos.end())
        {
            return -1 ;
        }
        CacheNode* node = pos[key] ;
        unlink(node) ;
        pushfront(head , node) ;
        return (int)node->val ;
    }

    void put(int key, int value) {
        // step 5 : key already there, new value, it becomes the newest
        if(pos.find(key) != pos.end())
        {
            CacheNode* node = pos[key] ;
            node->val = value ;
            unlink(node) ;
            pushfront(head , node) ;
            return ;
        }
        // step 6 : new key, evict the oldest first if the cache is full
        if((ll)pos.size() == cap)
        {
            CacheNode* oldest = tail->prev ;
            unlink(oldest) ;
            pos.erase(oldest->key) ;
            delete oldest ;
        }
        CacheNode* node = new CacheNode(key , value) ;
        pushfront(head , node) ;
        pos[key] = node ;
    }
};

Binary Tree General

104. Maximum Depth of Binary Tree Easy

LeetCode · cf/Maximum_Depth_of_Binary_Tree.cpp

// orz
// 104. Maximum Depth of Binary Tree · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given the root of a binary tree, return its maximum depth. the maximum depth
// is the number of nodes on the longest path from the root down to the farthest leaf.
// Example 1: root = [3,9,20,null,null,15,7] -> 3
// Example 2: root = [1,null,2]              -> 2
// Constraints: 0 <= number of nodes <= 10^4, -100 <= Node.val <= 100
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    rooted tree, depth(root) = 1, depth(child) = depth(parent) + 1.
//    return the max of depth(v) over all nodes v, and 0 for the empty tree.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every node, walk up to the root and count the nodes on the way.
//    O(n * h), a chain of 10^4 nodes gives about 5 * 10^7 steps.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the walk up from a child repeats the whole walk up from its parent.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    depth(child) = depth(parent) + 1, so one dfs from the root that carries depth
//    as a parameter knows every depth on arrival. that is my dfs(node , par , depth).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id 1 .. n and build g (tree template). empty tree -> 0.
//    step 2 : dfs(node , par , depth) from the root with depth 1, every child gets depth + 1.
//    step 3 : ans = the max depth seen at any node.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every node is reached exactly once and carries its true depth, so the max over
//    the dfs is the max depth. the deepest node is always a leaf, so this is the
//    longest root to leaf path.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids, g and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
ll ans ;
void dfs(ll node , ll par , ll depth)
{
    // step 3 : this stores the deepest depth seen so far in ans
    ans = max(ans , depth) ;
    // step 2 : every child is one level deeper
    for(auto child : g[node])
    {
        if(child != par)
        {
            dfs(child , node , depth + 1) ;
        }
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int maxDepth(TreeNode* root) {
        // step 1 : ids + g. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        ll rootid = encode(root) ;
        if(rootid == 0)
        {
            return 0 ; // empty tree
        }
        buildg() ;
        // step 2 : the root has depth 1
        ans = 0 ;
        dfs(rootid , 0 , 1) ;
        // step 3
        return ans ;
    }
};
100. Same Tree Easy

LeetCode · cf/Same_Tree.cpp

// orz
// 100. Same Tree · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given the roots of two binary trees p and q, return true if they are the same.
// two trees are the same when they have the same shape and the nodes in the
// same places have the same values.
// Example 1: p = [1,2,3], q = [1,2,3]  -> true
// Example 2: p = [1,2], q = [1,null,2] -> false
// Example 3: p = [1,2,1], q = [1,1,2]  -> false
// Constraints: 0 <= number of nodes in each tree <= 100, -10^4 <= Node.val <= 10^4
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    p = q when both are empty, or both are non-empty with val(p) = val(q),
//    left(p) = left(q) and right(p) = right(q).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every node of p, write its path from the root as moves L / R, walk the same
//    moves from the root of q, and check that a node is there with the same value.
//    then the same from q into p, because q may have extra nodes. O(n * h).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the walk to a node repeats the whole walk to its parent.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    encode numbers the nodes in preorder (node, then its left subtree, then its right subtree),
//    and that numbering depends only on the shape. two trees with the same shape get the
//    same ids at the same places, so "the node at the same place" is the node with the same id.
//    so same tree <=> same n, and for every id : same lc, same rc, same value.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : encode p (tree template, no g here: only left vs right matters).
//             keep a copy of its n, ptr, lc, rc.
//    step 2 : reset the globals and encode q.
//    step 3 : different n -> false.
//    step 4 : for every id 1 .. n : lc, rc and val must match, else false.
//    step 5 : everything matched -> true. (both empty: n = 0 on both sides -> true)
//
// 6. Why it works
//    preorder ids depend only on the shape, so same shape -> same ids, same lc / rc.
//    the other way, lc / rc read from id 1 rebuild the whole shape, so same lc / rc -> same shape.
//    with equal values at every id, the two trees are equal.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids and the copy.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
class Solution {
public:
    bool isSameTree(TreeNode* p, TreeNode* q) {
        // step 1 : ids for p. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        encode(p) ;
        ll np = n ;
        vector < TreeNode* > ptrp = ptr ;
        vector < ll > lcp = lc ;
        vector < ll > rcp = rc ;
        // step 2 : ids for q
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        encode(q) ;
        // step 3 : different number of nodes
        if(np != n)
        {
            return false ;
        }
        // step 4 : the same id is the same place in both trees
        for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
        {
            if(lcp[id] != lc[id] || rcp[id] != rc[id])
            {
                return false ; // different shape
            }
            else if(ptrp[id]->val != ptr[id]->val)
            {
                return false ; // same place, different value
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 5
        return true ;
    }
};
226. Invert Binary Tree Easy

LeetCode · cf/Invert_Binary_Tree.cpp

// orz
// 226. Invert Binary Tree · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given the root of a binary tree, invert it (mirror it left to right), and return its root.
// Example 1: root = [4,2,7,1,3,6,9] -> [4,7,2,9,6,3,1]
// Example 2: root = [2,1,3]         -> [2,3,1]
// Example 3: root = []              -> []
// Constraints: 0 <= number of nodes <= 100, -100 <= Node.val <= 100
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    for every node v : new left(v) = old right(v), new right(v) = old left(v).
//    same nodes, only the child pointers change.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every node, find it again by walking its L / R path from the root
//    (paths written down before anything changes), and swap its two children there.
//    O(n * h).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the swap at a node needs nothing but the node and its two old children,
//    so every walk from the root is wasted.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    the mirror is a local rule: every node swaps its own two children, and no node
//    cares what the others do. so I only need the list of all nodes with their OLD
//    children, and that is exactly ptr / lc / rc from the ids. lc / rc are read before
//    any pointer changes, so the order of the rewiring does not matter.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id (tree template, no g here). lc / rc keep the OLD children.
//    step 2 : for every id : ptr[id]->left = ptr[rc[id]], ptr[id]->right = ptr[lc[id]].
//             ptr[0] = NULL, so a missing child stays missing.
//    step 3 : return root, the root node itself does not move. (empty tree: n = 0,
//             step 2 does nothing, NULL comes back)
//
// 6. Why it works
//    mirror(v) = v with mirror(old right) on the left and mirror(old left) on the right.
//    from the leaves up, swapping at every node builds exactly that. lc / rc were saved
//    before the first change, so every swap uses the true old children.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
class Solution {
public:
    TreeNode* invertTree(TreeNode* root) {
        // step 1 : ids. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        encode(root) ;
        // step 2 : swap the children of every node through ptr
        for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
        {
            ptr[id]->left = ptr[rc[id]] ;
            ptr[id]->right = ptr[lc[id]] ;
        }
        // step 3
        return root ;
    }
};
101. Symmetric Tree Easy

LeetCode · cf/Symmetric_Tree.cpp

// orz
// 101. Symmetric Tree · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given the root of a binary tree, check whether it is a mirror of itself
// (symmetric around its center).
// Example 1: root = [1,2,2,3,4,4,3]       -> true
// Example 2: root = [1,2,2,null,3,null,3] -> false
// Constraints: 1 <= number of nodes <= 1000, -100 <= Node.val <= 100
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    mirror(a , b) is true when both are empty, or both are non-empty with val(a) = val(b),
//    mirror(left(a) , right(b)) and mirror(right(a) , left(b)).
//    answer = mirror(left(root) , right(root)).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    make an inverted copy of the tree (Invert Binary Tree), then run Same Tree on
//    the tree and its copy. O(n), but n new nodes and two extra passes.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the copy. the mirror image of the left half is only needed to be compared with
//    the right half, and that comparison can look at the two halves directly.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    symmetric <=> the left subtree is the mirror of the right subtree.
//    walk a PAIR (a , b) down together: the outside pair (left of a , right of b) and the
//    inside pair (right of a , left of b) must be mirrors again. left vs right matters,
//    so this walks lc / rc of the ids, not g.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id (tree template, no g here).
//    step 2 : mirror(a , b) : both 0 (empty) -> true.
//    step 3 : only one of them 0, or ptr[a]->val != ptr[b]->val -> false.
//    step 4 : else mirror(lc[a] , rc[b]) and mirror(rc[a] , lc[b]).
//    step 5 : answer = mirror(lc[root] , rc[root]).
//
// 6. Why it works
//    step 2 - 4 are the mathematical statement word for word, and every call moves both
//    a and b one level down, so it ends. every node is in at most one call.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
bool mirror(ll a , ll b)
{
    // this returns true if the subtree of id a is the mirror image of the subtree of id b
    // step 2 : both empty
    if(a == 0 && b == 0)
    {
        return true ;
    }
    // step 3 : only one empty, or different values
    if(a == 0 || b == 0)
    {
        return false ;
    }
    if(ptr[a]->val != ptr[b]->val)
    {
        return false ;
    }
    // step 4 : outside pair and inside pair
    bool outside = mirror(lc[a] , rc[b]) ;
    bool inside = mirror(rc[a] , lc[b]) ;
    return outside && inside ;
}
class Solution {
public:
    bool isSymmetric(TreeNode* root) {
        // step 1 : ids. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        ll rootid = encode(root) ;
        // step 5 : the two halves must mirror each other
        return mirror(lc[rootid] , rc[rootid]) ;
    }
};
105. Construct Binary Tree from Preorder and Inorder Traversal Medium

LeetCode · cf/Construct_Binary_Tree_from_Preorder_and_Inorder_Traversal.cpp

// orz
// 105. Construct Binary Tree from Preorder and Inorder Traversal · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given two integer arrays preorder and inorder, the preorder and the inorder
// traversal of the same binary tree, build that tree and return its root.
// Example 1: preorder = [3,9,20,15,7], inorder = [9,3,15,20,7] -> [3,9,20,null,null,15,7]
// Example 2: preorder = [-1], inorder = [-1]                   -> [-1]
// Constraints: 1 <= n = preorder.length <= 3000, inorder.length == n,
//              -3000 <= values <= 3000, all values are distinct,
//              both arrays are guaranteed to be traversals of the same tree.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    preorder(T) = root, preorder(left), preorder(right).
//    inorder(T)  = inorder(left), root, inorder(right).
//    find the tree T with these two sequences (values distinct, so T is unique).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    root = preorder[0]. scan inorder for it: everything before it is the left subtree,
//    everything after it the right subtree. cut preorder by those sizes, copy the four
//    pieces into new arrays and recurse on each side. O(n^2) on a chain, from the scans and the copies.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the scan for a value in inorder gives the same answer every time (values are distinct),
//    and the copies only pass on pieces that are already contiguous.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    every subtree is a contiguous range of inorder. and its nodes come in preorder in
//    exactly the order the recursion asks for them: root, then the whole left subtree,
//    then the whole right subtree. so pass only the inorder range [l , r], keep one global
//    index pi into preorder that moves forward by 1 for every new node, and precompute
//    pos[value] = index of value in inorder.
//    that is a plain build(l , r), like my segment tree build(id , l , r).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : pos[inorder[i]] = i for every i, pi = 0 (reset every global).
//    step 2 : build(l , r) returns the root of the subtree whose inorder is inorder[l .. r].
//             l > r -> empty, NULL.
//    step 3 : the root is preorder[pi], the next unused preorder value. pi++.
//    step 4 : m = pos[root value]. left = build(l , m - 1), THEN right = build(m + 1 , r).
//             left first, because preorder lists the whole left subtree before the right one.
//    step 5 : return build(0 , n - 1).
//
// 6. Why it works
//    inorder puts the root between its left and its right subtree, so m splits [l , r] exactly.
//    build makes nodes in the order root, left, right, the same order as preorder,
//    so preorder[pi] is always the root of the range being built.
//    time O(n log n) with the map, space O(n) for pos and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
vector < ll > pre ;    // this stores preorder
map < ll , ll > pos ;  // pos[value] = index of value in inorder
ll pi ;                // index of the next unused value in pre
TreeNode* build(ll l , ll r)
{
    // this returns the root of the subtree whose inorder is inorder[l .. r]
    // step 2 : empty range
    if(l > r)
    {
        return NULL ;
    }
    // step 3 : the next preorder value is the root
    TreeNode* cur = new TreeNode(pre[pi]) ;
    pi++ ;
    // step 4 : split inorder at the root, left subtree first
    ll m = pos[cur->val] ;
    cur->left = build(l , m - 1) ;
    cur->right = build(m + 1 , r) ;
    return cur ;
}
class Solution {
public:
    TreeNode* buildTree(vector<int>& preorder, vector<int>& inorder) {
        // step 1 : reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        ll n = preorder.size() ;
        pre.assign(n , 0) ;
        pos.clear() ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            pre[i] = preorder[i] ;
            pos[inorder[i]] = i ;
        }
        pi = 0 ;
        // step 5
        return build(0 , n - 1) ;
    }
};
106. Construct Binary Tree from Inorder and Postorder Traversal Medium

LeetCode · cf/Construct_Binary_Tree_from_Inorder_and_Postorder_Traversal.cpp

// orz
// 106. Construct Binary Tree from Inorder and Postorder Traversal · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given two integer arrays inorder and postorder, the inorder and the postorder
// traversal of the same binary tree, build that tree and return its root.
// Example 1: inorder = [9,3,15,20,7], postorder = [9,15,7,20,3] -> [3,9,20,null,null,15,7]
// Example 2: inorder = [-1], postorder = [-1]                   -> [-1]
// Constraints: 1 <= n = inorder.length <= 3000, postorder.length == n,
//              -3000 <= values <= 3000, all values are distinct,
//              both arrays are guaranteed to be traversals of the same tree.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    inorder(T)   = inorder(left), root, inorder(right).
//    postorder(T) = postorder(left), postorder(right), root.
//    find the tree T with these two sequences (values distinct, so T is unique).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    root = the last value of postorder. scan inorder for it: everything before it is the
//    left subtree, everything after it the right subtree. cut postorder by those sizes,
//    copy the four pieces into new arrays and recurse. O(n^2) on a chain.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the scan for a value in inorder gives the same answer every time (values are distinct),
//    and the copies only pass on pieces that are already contiguous.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    read postorder BACKWARDS: root, then the whole right subtree, then the whole left subtree.
//    that is a preorder with right before left. so it is Construct from Preorder and Inorder
//    mirrored: pass only the inorder range [l , r], keep one global index pi that walks
//    postorder from the back, pos[value] = index of value in inorder, and build the RIGHT
//    subtree first. a plain build(l , r), like my segment tree build(id , l , r).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : pos[inorder[i]] = i for every i, pi = n - 1 (reset every global).
//    step 2 : build(l , r) returns the root of the subtree whose inorder is inorder[l .. r].
//             l > r -> empty, NULL.
//    step 3 : the root is post[pi], the last unused postorder value. pi--.
//    step 4 : m = pos[root value]. right = build(m + 1 , r), THEN left = build(l , m - 1).
//             right first, because backwards postorder lists the whole right subtree before the left one.
//    step 5 : return build(0 , n - 1).
//
// 6. Why it works
//    inorder puts the root between its left and its right subtree, so m splits [l , r] exactly.
//    build makes nodes in the order root, right, left, the same order as postorder read
//    backwards, so post[pi] is always the root of the range being built.
//    time O(n log n) with the map, space O(n) for pos and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
vector < ll > post ;   // this stores postorder
map < ll , ll > pos ;  // pos[value] = index of value in inorder
ll pi ;                // index of the last unused value in post, it walks backwards
TreeNode* build(ll l , ll r)
{
    // this returns the root of the subtree whose inorder is inorder[l .. r]
    // step 2 : empty range
    if(l > r)
    {
        return NULL ;
    }
    // step 3 : the last unused postorder value is the root
    TreeNode* cur = new TreeNode(post[pi]) ;
    pi-- ;
    // step 4 : split inorder at the root, right subtree first
    ll m = pos[cur->val] ;
    cur->right = build(m + 1 , r) ;
    cur->left = build(l , m - 1) ;
    return cur ;
}
class Solution {
public:
    TreeNode* buildTree(vector<int>& inorder, vector<int>& postorder) {
        // step 1 : reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        ll n = inorder.size() ;
        post.assign(n , 0) ;
        pos.clear() ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            post[i] = postorder[i] ;
            pos[inorder[i]] = i ;
        }
        pi = n - 1 ;
        // step 5
        return build(0 , n - 1) ;
    }
};
117. Populating Next Right Pointers in Each Node II Medium

LeetCode · cf/Populating_Next_Right_Pointers_in_Each_Node_II.cpp

// orz
// 117. Populating Next Right Pointers in Each Node II · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given a binary tree whose nodes have val, left, right and next, point every
// next to the node right after it on the same level (left to right). the last
// node of a level gets next = NULL. at the start every next is NULL. return root.
// Example 1: root = [1,2,3,4,5,null,7] -> [1,#,2,3,#,4,5,7,#]   (# ends a level, read along next)
// Example 2: root = []                 -> []
// Constraints: 0 <= number of nodes <= 6000, -100 <= Node.val <= 100
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    for every depth d, let v1 .. vk be the nodes at depth d from left to right.
//    set next(vi) = v(i + 1) for i < k, and next(vk) = NULL.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every node u, look at every node of the same depth and keep the closest one
//    to the right of u (compare the root to node paths as strings, L < R).
//    O(n^2 * h).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the answer for u is just the one after u in the left to right list of its level,
//    and the brute force rebuilds that list for every node.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    the ids from encode are preorder: node, then its whole left subtree, then its whole right subtree.
//    two different nodes x, y on the same level are not ancestors of each other. if x is left of y,
//    they split at some node z with x under left(z) and y under right(z), and all of left(z)
//    gets its ids before right(z). so on one level, left to right = increasing id.
//    walk the ids 1 .. n once and keep last[d] = the last id seen at depth d: a new id at
//    depth d is exactly the next of last[d].
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id 1 .. n and build g (tree template, Node in place of TreeNode).
//             empty tree -> return root (NULL).
//    step 2 : dfs(node , par , depth) stores d[node], the root has depth 0.
//    step 3 : for id = 1 .. n in increasing order : if last[d[id]] != 0,
//             ptr[last[d[id]]]->next = ptr[id]. then last[d[id]] = id.
//    step 4 : the last node of every level keeps next = NULL (as given). return root.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    on every level, increasing id is left to right (the key breaking point), so
//    step 3 links every pair of neighbours on the level, and nothing else.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids, g, d, last and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/*
// Definition for a Node.
class Node {
public:
    int val;
    Node* left;
    Node* right;
    Node* next;

    Node() : val(0), left(NULL), right(NULL), next(NULL) {}

    Node(int _val) : val(_val), left(NULL), right(NULL), next(NULL) {}

    Node(int _val, Node* _left, Node* _right, Node* _next)
        : val(_val), left(_left), right(_right), next(_next) {}
};
*/
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md, Node in place of TreeNode) ----
ll n ;
vector < Node* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(Node* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
vector < ll > d ;  // d[id] = depth of node id, the root has depth 0
void dfs(ll node , ll par , ll depth)
{
    // step 2 : this stores the depth of node and of everything below it
    d[node] = depth ;
    for(auto child : g[node])
    {
        if(child != par)
        {
            dfs(child , node , depth + 1) ;
        }
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    Node* connect(Node* root) {
        // step 1 : ids + g. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        ll rootid = encode(root) ;
        if(rootid == 0)
        {
            return root ; // empty tree
        }
        buildg() ;
        // step 2 : depth of every node
        d.assign(n + 1 , 0) ;
        dfs(rootid , 0 , 0) ;
        // step 3 : on each level, increasing id = left to right
        vector < ll > last(n + 1 , 0) ; // last[depth] = last id seen at this depth, 0 = none yet
        for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
        {
            ll dep = d[id] ;
            if(last[dep] != 0)
            {
                ptr[last[dep]]->next = ptr[id] ;
            }
            last[dep] = id ;
        }
        // step 4
        return root ;
    }
};
114. Flatten Binary Tree to Linked List Medium

LeetCode · cf/Flatten_Binary_Tree_to_Linked_List.cpp

// orz
// 114. Flatten Binary Tree to Linked List · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given the root of a binary tree, flatten it into a "linked list" in place:
// the same nodes, right is the next pointer, left is always NULL, and the
// order of the list is the preorder of the tree.
// Example 1: root = [1,2,5,3,4,null,6] -> [1,null,2,null,3,null,4,null,5,null,6]
// Example 2: root = []                 -> []
// Example 3: root = [0]                -> [0]
// Constraints: 0 <= number of nodes <= 2000, -100 <= Node.val <= 100
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    let v1 .. vn be the nodes in preorder. set right(vi) = v(i + 1), right(vn) = NULL
//    and left(vi) = NULL for every i, without making new nodes.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    repeat : find the first node in preorder that still has a left child, walk to the last
//    node of that left subtree, hang the right subtree there, move the left subtree to the right.
//    every round starts again from the root : O(n^2).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    every round walks the tree again to find a preorder that never changes.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    the preorder is fixed before I change anything, and encode already numbers the nodes
//    in preorder (node, then its left subtree, then its right subtree). so ptr[1] .. ptr[n]
//    IS the list, and the whole job is rewiring ptr[id]->right = ptr[id + 1].
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id (tree template, no g here). ptr[id] = the id-th node in preorder.
//    step 2 : ptr[n + 1] = NULL, so the last node gets right = NULL like every other.
//    step 3 : for id = 1 .. n : ptr[id]->left = NULL, ptr[id]->right = ptr[id + 1].
//             (empty tree : n = 0, nothing to do)
//
// 6. Why it works
//    encode visits node, left subtree, right subtree, which is preorder, so ptr is the list order.
//    every id was given before the first pointer changed, so the rewiring can't spoil the walk.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
class Solution {
public:
    void flatten(TreeNode* root) {
        // step 1 : ids in preorder. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        encode(root) ;
        // step 2 : ptr[n + 1] = NULL ends the list
        ptr.push_back(NULL) ;
        // step 3 : chain ptr[1] .. ptr[n] through right
        for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
        {
            ptr[id]->left = NULL ;
            ptr[id]->right = ptr[id + 1] ;
        }
    }
};
112. Path Sum Easy

LeetCode · cf/Path_Sum.cpp

// orz
// 112. Path Sum · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given the root of a binary tree and an integer targetSum, return true if the tree
// has a root-to-leaf path whose values add up to targetSum. a leaf is a node with no children.
// Example 1: root = [5,4,8,11,null,13,4,7,2,null,null,null,1], targetSum = 22 -> true   (5 -> 4 -> 11 -> 2)
// Example 2: root = [1,2,3], targetSum = 5                                    -> false
// Example 3: root = [], targetSum = 0                                         -> false  (no path at all)
// Constraints: 0 <= number of nodes <= 5000, -1000 <= Node.val <= 1000, -1000 <= targetSum <= 1000
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    is there a leaf v with (sum of w(u) over u on the path root -> v) = targetSum ?
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every leaf, walk up to the root adding the values. O(n * h).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    leaves under the same node add up the same top part of the path again.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    sum(root -> child) = sum(root -> node) + w(child). carry the sum down as a dfs
//    parameter, exactly like depth in my dfs(node , par , depth).
//    a leaf in g = a node whose only neighbour is par, so no child call happens.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id 1 .. n and build g (tree template). empty tree -> false.
//    step 2 : dfs(node , par , sum) with sum = sum above node. sum += w(node) is root .. node.
//    step 3 : count the children while going down. children == 0 means node is a leaf:
//             if sum == targetSum, found = true.
//    step 4 : return found.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every root to leaf path is one branch of the dfs and arrives at its leaf with its
//    exact sum. |sum| <= 5000 * 1000, no overflow.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids, g and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
ll target ;
bool found ;
void dfs(ll node , ll par , ll sum)
{
    // step 2 : now sum = sum of the values from the root down to node
    sum += ptr[node]->val ;
    ll children = 0 ;
    for(auto child : g[node])
    {
        if(child != par)
        {
            children++ ;
            dfs(child , node , sum) ;
        }
    }
    // step 3 : no children means node is a leaf, the path root -> node is complete
    if(children == 0 && sum == target)
    {
        found = true ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    bool hasPathSum(TreeNode* root, int targetSum) {
        // step 1 : ids + g. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        ll rootid = encode(root) ;
        if(rootid == 0)
        {
            return false ; // empty tree has no root-to-leaf path
        }
        buildg() ;
        // step 2 : nothing above the root, sum starts at 0
        target = targetSum ;
        found = false ;
        dfs(rootid , 0 , 0) ;
        // step 4
        return found ;
    }
};
129. Sum Root to Leaf Numbers Medium

LeetCode · cf/Sum_Root_to_Leaf_Numbers.cpp

// orz
// 129. Sum Root to Leaf Numbers · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// every node holds one digit 0 .. 9. every root-to-leaf path is read as a number,
// e.g. the path 1 -> 2 -> 3 is the number 123. return the sum of all these numbers.
// a leaf is a node with no children. the answer fits in a 32-bit integer.
// Example 1: root = [1,2,3]       -> 25    (12 + 13)
// Example 2: root = [4,9,0,5,1]   -> 1026  (495 + 491 + 40)
// Constraints: 1 <= number of nodes <= 1000, 0 <= Node.val <= 9, depth of the tree <= 10
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    for a leaf v with path root = u1, u2, .. , uk = v, num(v) = digits w(u1) w(u2) .. w(uk)
//    = sum of w(ui) * 10^(k - i). return the sum of num(v) over all leaves v.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every leaf, walk up to the root, collect the digits, reverse them, build the number.
//    O(n * h).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    leaves under the same node read the same top digits again.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    num(root -> child) = num(root -> node) * 10 + w(child): appending a digit is * 10 + digit.
//    carry the number down as a dfs parameter, exactly like depth in my dfs(node , par , depth).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id 1 .. n and build g (tree template).
//    step 2 : dfs(node , par , num) with num = number above node. num = num * 10 + w(node).
//    step 3 : count the children while going down. children == 0 means node is a leaf: ans += num.
//    step 4 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every root to leaf path is one branch of the dfs and arrives at its leaf with its exact
//    number. depth <= 10, so a number is < 10^10, kept in ll, and the total fits in int (given).
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids, g and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
ll ans ;
void dfs(ll node , ll par , ll num)
{
    // step 2 : now num = the digits from the root down to node
    num = num * 10 + ptr[node]->val ;
    ll children = 0 ;
    for(auto child : g[node])
    {
        if(child != par)
        {
            children++ ;
            dfs(child , node , num) ;
        }
    }
    // step 3 : no children means node is a leaf, num is a whole root-to-leaf number
    if(children == 0)
    {
        ans += num ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int sumNumbers(TreeNode* root) {
        // step 1 : ids + g. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        ll rootid = encode(root) ;
        buildg() ;
        // step 2 : nothing above the root, num starts at 0
        ans = 0 ;
        dfs(rootid , 0 , 0) ;
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};
124. Binary Tree Maximum Path Sum Hard

LeetCode · cf/Binary_Tree_Maximum_Path_Sum.cpp

// orz
// 124. Binary Tree Maximum Path Sum · LeetCode · Hard
//
// Question:
// a path in a binary tree is a sequence of nodes where each pair of adjacent
// nodes has an edge between them, and a node appears at most once. the path
// does not need to pass through the root. the path sum is the sum of the node
// values on the path. given the root, return the maximum path sum of any
// non-empty path.
// Example 1: root = [1,2,3]                  -> 6   (2 -> 1 -> 3)
// Example 2: root = [-10,9,20,null,null,15,7] -> 42  (15 -> 20 -> 7)
// Constraints: 1 <= number of nodes <= 3 * 10^4, -1000 <= Node.val <= 1000
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    tree with values w(v). maximise the sum of w(v) over v on P,
//    over every simple path P with at least one node.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    run a dfs from every node u, and every node v it reaches closes a path u -> v.
//    n dfs runs of O(n) each = O(n^2) = 9 * 10^8. too slow.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    paths with different ends share the same downward pieces, and every dfs
//    sums those pieces again.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    root the tree. every path has exactly one highest node top (the one closest to the root).
//    from top, the path goes down into at most two different children, and each side
//    is a downward path (an "arm"). so
//    best path with highest node top = w(top) + max(0 , best left arm) + max(0 , best right arm),
//    because a negative arm is just dropped.
//    now I only need indp[v] = best downward path starting at v, for every v:
//    the same in-dp as Maximum White Subtree (max(0LL , indp[child])).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : give every node an id 1 .. n and build g (tree template), so my dfs(node , par) works.
//    step 2 : indp[node] = w(node) + max(0 , best indp[child]).
//             best downward path that starts at node: node alone, or node plus its best arm.
//    step 3 : at the same node, the best path whose highest node is node =
//             w(node) + sum over children of max(0 , indp[child]). (at most 2 children)
//    step 4 : ans = max of step 3 over all nodes. ans starts at -INF, because all values can be negative.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every path has exactly one highest node, so it is counted at that node in step 3.
//    the two arms lie in different subtrees, so they can't share a node, and each can be
//    chosen on its own. a negative arm can be dropped because the path may be the node alone.
//    time O(n), space O(n) for the ids, g and the recursion.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
/**
 * Definition for a binary tree node.
 * struct TreeNode {
 *     int val;
 *     TreeNode *left;
 *     TreeNode *right;
 *     TreeNode() : val(0), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x) : val(x), left(nullptr), right(nullptr) {}
 *     TreeNode(int x, TreeNode *left, TreeNode *right) : val(x), left(left), right(right) {}
 * };
 */
// ---- tree template (TEMPLATES.md) ----
ll n ;
vector < TreeNode* > ptr ;  // ptr[id] = the tree node with this id
vector < ll > lc , rc ;     // lc[id] / rc[id] = id of the left / right child, 0 means no child
vector < vector < ll > > g ;
ll encode(TreeNode* cur)
{
    // this gives cur and everything below it an id, and returns the id of cur (0 if cur is NULL)
    if(cur == NULL)
    {
        return 0 ;
    }
    n++ ;
    ll id = n ;
    ptr.push_back(cur) ;
    lc.push_back(0) ;
    rc.push_back(0) ;
    ll l = encode(cur->left) ;
    ll r = encode(cur->right) ;
    lc[id] = l ;
    rc[id] = r ;
    return id ;
}
void buildg()
{
    // this turns lc / rc into my adjacency list g
    g.assign(n + 1 , vector < ll > ()) ;
    for(ll id = 1 ; id <= n ; id++)
    {
        if(lc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(lc[id]) ;
            g[lc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
        if(rc[id] != 0)
        {
            g[id].push_back(rc[id]) ;
            g[rc[id]].push_back(id) ;
        }
    }
}
// ---- end of tree template ----
vector < ll > indp ;
ll ans ;
void dfsin(ll node , ll par)
{
    // indp[node] = best sum of a downward path that starts at node
    ll w = ptr[node]->val ;
    ll bestarm = 0 ;  // best child arm, 0 means take no arm
    ll botharms = 0 ; // sum of every child arm that helps
    for(auto child : g[node])
    {
        if(child != par)
        {
            dfsin(child , node) ;
            bestarm = max(bestarm , indp[child]) ;
            botharms += max(0LL , indp[child]) ;
        }
    }
    // step 2 : node plus its best arm
    indp[node] = w + bestarm ;
    // step 3 : best path whose highest node is node
    ans = max(ans , w + botharms) ;
}
class Solution {
public:
    int maxPathSum(TreeNode* root) {
        // step 1 : ids + g. reset every global, LeetCode keeps them between test cases
        n = 0 ;
        ptr.assign(1 , NULL) ;
        lc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        rc.assign(1 , 0) ;
        ll rootid = encode(root) ;
        buildg() ;
        // step 4 : all values can be negative, so start at -INF
        ans = -INF ;
        indp.assign(n + 1 , 0) ;
        dfsin(rootid , 0) ;
        return ans ;
    }
};
173. Binary Search Tree Iterator Medium

LeetCode · cf/Binary_Search_Tree_Iterator.cpp

// orz
// 173. Binary Search Tree Iterator · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// implement the class BSTIterator, which walks the inorder (sorted) order of a BST:
// BSTIterator(root) : starts the iterator just before the smallest value.
// hasNext()         : true if there is a next value in the inorder order.
// next()            : moves to the next value and returns it.
// next() is only called when there is a next value.
