//    the normal blocks are exactly Maximum Subarray (53), which is O(n). the brute force redoes
//    that work for every start just to also cover the wrapping blocks.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    a wrapping block = the whole array MINUS the middle block nums[r + 1 .. l - 1] it skips.
//    so best wrapping sum = total - (smallest sum of a normal block). that is Kadane again, with min.
//    the skipped middle must not be the whole array (the answer is non-empty). that only matters
//    when every number is negative: then the best normal block (the largest number) is the answer.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : dp "ending at i" twice, as in 53.
//             mx(i) = the largest sum of a block ending at i  = max(mx(i - 1) + nums[i] , nums[i]).
//             mn(i) = the smallest sum of a block ending at i = min(mn(i - 1) + nums[i] , nums[i]).
//             CHECK : i = 0, both are nums[0].
//    step 2 : bottom up i from 0 to n - 1 with rolling rows: [(i + 1) & 1] holds i - 1, [i & 1] gets i.
//             best = max of mx(i), worst = min of mn(i), total = sum of nums.
//    step 3 : best < 0 means every number is negative : return best.
//    step 4 : else return max(best , total - worst) (normal block, or wrapping block).
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every circular block is normal (counted in best) or wrapping (= total - a normal middle block,
//    so the best wrapping one is total - worst). if best >= 0 and worst happens to be the whole array,
//    total - worst = 0 <= best, so the empty answer never wins. if best < 0, all numbers are negative,
//    and adding more numbers only lowers a sum, so the best block is one number, which best already is.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// ll recmx(ll i)
// {
//     // this returns the largest sum of a block that ends exactly at index i
//     if(i == 0)
//     {
//         return nums[0] ;
//     }
//     ll extend = recmx(i - 1) + nums[i] ;
//     ll restart = nums[i] ;
//     return max(extend , restart) ;
// }
// ll recmn(ll i)
// {
//     // this returns the smallest sum of a block that ends exactly at index i
//     if(i == 0)
//     {
//         return nums[0] ;
//     }
//     ll extend = recmn(i - 1) + nums[i] ;
//     ll restart = nums[i] ;
//     return min(extend , restart) ;
// }
class Solution {
public:
    int maxSubarraySumCircular(vector<int>& nums) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 1 : two slots each, mx[i & 1] / mn[i & 1]
        vector < ll > mx(2 , 0) , mn(2 , 0) ;
        ll best = -INF ;  // largest normal block
        ll worst = INF ;  // smallest normal block
        ll total = 0 ;
        // step 2 : i from 0 to n - 1
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            total += nums[i] ;
            if(i == 0)
            {
                mx[i & 1] = nums[0] ;
                mn[i & 1] = nums[0] ;
                best = max(best , mx[i & 1]) ;
                worst = min(worst , mn[i & 1]) ;
                continue ;
            }
            // extend the block ending at i - 1, or restart at i
            ll extendmx = mx[(i + 1) & 1] + nums[i] ; // mx[(i + 1) & 1] holds mx(i - 1)
            ll extendmn = mn[(i + 1) & 1] + nums[i] ;
            ll restart = nums[i] ;
            mx[i & 1] = max(extendmx , restart) ;
            mn[i & 1] = min(extendmn , restart) ;
            best = max(best , mx[i & 1]) ;
            worst = min(worst , mn[i & 1]) ;
        }
        // step 3 : all numbers negative, the wrapping formula would give the empty block
        if(best < 0)
        {
            return best ;
        }
        else
        {
            // step 4 : normal block or wrapping block
            return max(best , total - worst) ;
        }
    }
};

Binary Search

35. Search Insert Position Easy

LeetCode · cf/Search_Insert_Position.cpp

// orz
// 35. Search Insert Position · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given a sorted array of distinct integers nums and a target value, return the
// index of target if it is in nums. if it is not, return the index where it would
// be inserted to keep nums sorted. you must write an O(log n) algorithm.
// Example 1: nums = [1,3,5,6], target = 5 -> 2
// Example 2: nums = [1,3,5,6], target = 2 -> 1
// Example 3: nums = [1,3,5,6], target = 7 -> 4
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 10^4, -10^4 <= nums[i], target <= 10^4,
//              nums has distinct values sorted in ascending order.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    find the smallest index i in [0, n] with nums[i] >= target,
//    where nums[n] counts as +infinity (so i = n means "after everything").
//
// 2. Brute force
//    walk i = 0 .. n - 1 and stop at the first nums[i] >= target.
//    O(n), and the judge asks for O(log n).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it ignores that nums is sorted. one look at nums[mid] already answers
//    the question for a whole half of the array.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    check(i) = "nums[i] >= target" reads false false .. false true true .. true
//    along the array, because nums is sorted. the answer is the first index where
//    check is true: my binary search template, lo / hi / ans.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : check(i) returns true if nums[i] >= target.
//    step 2 : lo = 0, hi = n - 1, ans = n. ans = n covers "target is bigger than everything".
//    step 3 : mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2.
//             check(mid) true  -> mid is a candidate, ans = mid, look left: hi = mid - 1.
//             check(mid) false -> the first true is right of mid: lo = mid + 1.
//    step 4 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    everything left of the first true index is < target and everything from it on
//    is >= target, so it is exactly where target sits (if present) or where it goes.
//    each round halves [lo, hi]. time O(log n), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 1
bool check(ll i , vector<int>& nums , ll target)
{
    // this returns true if nums[i] >= target
    if(nums[i] >= target)
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int searchInsert(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 2
        ll lo = 0 , hi = n - 1 , ans = n ;
        // step 3 : first index where check is true
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(check(mid , nums , target))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};
74. Search a 2D Matrix Medium

LeetCode · cf/Search_a_2D_Matrix.cpp

// orz
// 74. Search a 2D Matrix · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// you are given an m x n integer matrix with two properties: each row is sorted
// in non-decreasing order, and the first integer of each row is greater than the
// last integer of the previous row. given an integer target, return true if
// target is in the matrix and false otherwise. you must write a solution in
// O(log(m * n)) time.
// Example 1: matrix = [[1,3,5,7],[10,11,16,20],[23,30,34,60]], target = 3  -> true
// Example 2: matrix = [[1,3,5,7],[10,11,16,20],[23,30,34,60]], target = 13 -> false
// Constraints: 1 <= m, n <= 100, -10^4 <= matrix[i][j], target <= 10^4
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    read the matrix row by row into one list a[0 .. m * n - 1], so cell k is
//    a[k] = matrix[k / n][k % n]. the two properties say a is sorted.
//    answer: does some k have a[k] = target?
//
// 2. Brute force
//    look at all m * n cells. O(m * n) = 10^4, and the judge asks for O(log(m * n)).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the whole matrix is one sorted list in disguise, and it never uses that.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    don't copy the matrix into a list: cell k is matrix[k / n][k % n].
//    k / n = how many full rows of n come before cell k = its row, k % n = what is left = its column.
//    then it is Search Insert Position on a: first k with a[k] >= target, then look at that cell.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : check(k) returns true if a[k] = matrix[k / n][k % n] >= target.
//             false .. false true .. true along k because a is sorted.
//    step 2 : lo = 0, hi = m * n - 1, ans = m * n (m * n means "every cell is < target").
//    step 3 : the binary search template for the first k where check is true.
//    step 4 : target is in the matrix exactly when ans < m * n and a[ans] = target.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    a is sorted, so if target is anywhere it sits at the first cell >= target.
//    time O(log(m * n)), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 1
bool check(ll k , vector<vector<int>>& matrix , ll target)
{
    // this returns true if cell k (reading row by row) is >= target
    ll n = matrix[0].size() ;
    ll row = k / n ;
    ll col = k % n ;
    if(matrix[row][col] >= target)
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    bool searchMatrix(vector<vector<int>>& matrix, int target) {
        ll m = matrix.size() ;
        ll n = matrix[0].size() ;
        // step 2
        ll lo = 0 , hi = m * n - 1 , ans = m * n ;
        // step 3 : first cell where check is true
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(check(mid , matrix , target))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        if(ans == m * n)
        {
            return false ;
        }
        if(matrix[ans / n][ans % n] == target)
        {
            return true ;
        }
        else
        {
            return false ;
        }
    }
};
162. Find Peak Element Medium

LeetCode · cf/Find_Peak_Element.cpp

// orz
// 162. Find Peak Element · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// a peak element is an element that is strictly greater than its neighbours.
// given a 0-indexed integer array nums, find a peak element and return its index.
// if there are several peaks, return the index of any of them. imagine
// nums[-1] = nums[n] = -infinity, so an element at the border only has to beat
// its one real neighbour. you must write an O(log n) algorithm.
// Example 1: nums = [1,2,3,1]         -> 2   (3 is a peak)
// Example 2: nums = [1,2,1,3,5,6,4]   -> 1 or 5   (2 is a peak, 6 is a peak)
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 1000, -2^31 <= nums[i] <= 2^31 - 1,
//              nums[i] != nums[i + 1] for every valid i.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    with nums[-1] = nums[n] = -infinity, find any i with nums[i - 1] < nums[i] > nums[i + 1].
//
// 2. Brute force
//    test every i against its two neighbours. O(n), the judge asks for O(log n).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    one comparison nums[i] vs nums[i + 1] already tells which side surely has a peak,
//    and it throws that away.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    call check(i) true if i is the last index or nums[i] > nums[i + 1] ("the road goes down after i").
//    check is NOT false .. true over the whole array (it can switch many times), but my template
//    still works with one fact: check(n - 1) is true. when the template stops it returns an index
//    ans with check(ans) true and check(ans - 1) false (or ans = 0). that ans is a peak:
//    check(ans - 1) false -> nums[ans - 1] < nums[ans] (neighbours differ),
//    check(ans) true      -> nums[ans] > nums[ans + 1] or ans is the last index.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : check(i) returns true if i = n - 1 or nums[i] > nums[i + 1].
//    step 2 : lo = 0, hi = n - 1, ans = n - 1.
//    step 3 : the binary search template: check(mid) true -> ans = mid, hi = mid - 1,
//             false -> lo = mid + 1.
//    step 4 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    why the template lands on a switch false -> true:
//    hi only changes together with ans (hi = ans - 1), and lo only changes to mid + 1 right after
//    check(mid) was false. ans is set at least once, because if it never were, hi stays n - 1, so lo
//    would have to pass n - 1, which needs check(n - 1) false. impossible.
//    so at the end lo = hi + 1 = ans, and lo is either 0 or (an index with check false) + 1.
//    so check(ans) is true and check(ans - 1) is false (or ans = 0, where nums[-1] = -infinity).
//    by the key breaking point (4.) that is a peak. time O(log n), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 1
bool check(ll i , vector<int>& nums)
{
    // this returns true if the road goes down after i (or i is the last index)
    ll n = nums.size() ;
    if(i == n - 1)
    {
        return true ;
    }
    if(nums[i] > nums[i + 1])
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int findPeakElement(vector<int>& nums) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 2 : check(n - 1) is always true
        ll lo = 0 , hi = n - 1 , ans = n - 1 ;
        // step 3
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(check(mid , nums))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};
33. Search in Rotated Sorted Array Medium

LeetCode · cf/Search_in_Rotated_Sorted_Array.cpp

// orz
// 33. Search in Rotated Sorted Array · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// there is an integer array nums sorted in ascending order with distinct values.
// before being passed to you, nums is possibly left rotated at an unknown index k
// (1 <= k < n), so it becomes [nums[k], .., nums[n-1], nums[0], .., nums[k-1]].
// for example [0,1,2,4,5,6,7] rotated at k = 3 becomes [4,5,6,7,0,1,2].
// given nums after the possible rotation and an integer target, return the index
// of target in nums, or -1 if it is not in nums. you must write an O(log n) algorithm.
// Example 1: nums = [4,5,6,7,0,1,2], target = 0 -> 4
// Example 2: nums = [4,5,6,7,0,1,2], target = 3 -> -1
// Example 3: nums = [1], target = 0             -> -1
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 5000, -10^4 <= nums[i], target <= 10^4, all values unique,
//              nums is an ascending array that is possibly rotated.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    nums = (piece A)(piece B), both ascending, every value of A bigger than every value
//    of B (A may be empty). find the i with nums[i] = target, or say there is none.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    scan all n values. O(n), the judge asks for O(log n).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    each piece is sorted, and a sorted piece can be searched in O(log n).
//    the only thing in the way is not knowing where the pieces split.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    split the problem into two of my standard binary searches:
//    (a) find where piece B starts: that is exactly Find Minimum in Rotated Sorted Array,
//        the first index with nums[i] <= nums[n - 1].
//    (b) target > nums[n - 1] can only be in A (B is all <= nums[n - 1]), otherwise only in B.
//        that piece is sorted: Search Insert Position on it, the first index with nums[i] >= target.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : checkpivot(i) returns true if nums[i] <= nums[n - 1] (i is in piece B).
//             binary search template, ans = n - 1 at the start -> p = first index of B.
//    step 2 : choose the piece. target > nums[n - 1] -> [0 , p - 1] (piece A, may be empty),
//             else -> [p , n - 1] (piece B).
//    step 3 : check(i) returns true if nums[i] >= target. binary search template on the
//             chosen piece, ans = -1 at the start (-1 = no index in the piece is >= target).
//    step 4 : if ans != -1 and nums[ans] = target return ans, else return -1.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    step 1 is the Find Minimum argument: A is all > nums[n - 1], B is all <= nums[n - 1],
//    so checkpivot is false .. false true .. true.
//    step 2 never throws target away: a value > nums[n - 1] can't be in B, a value <= nums[n - 1]
//    can't be in A. inside one sorted piece check is false .. true, and target, if present,
//    is the first value >= target. time O(log n) (two binary searches), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 1
bool checkpivot(ll i , vector<int>& nums)
{
    // this returns true if index i is in piece B (nums[i] <= the last element)
    ll n = nums.size() ;
    if(nums[i] <= nums[n - 1])
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
// step 3
bool check(ll i , vector<int>& nums , ll target)
{
    // this returns true if nums[i] >= target
    if(nums[i] >= target)
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int search(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 1 : p = first index of piece B
        ll lo = 0 , hi = n - 1 , ans = n - 1 ;
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(checkpivot(mid , nums))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
        }
        ll p = ans ;
        // step 2 : choose the piece target can be in
        if(target > nums[n - 1])
        {
            lo = 0 ;
            hi = p - 1 ;
        }
        else
        {
            lo = p ;
            hi = n - 1 ;
        }
        // step 3 : first index in the piece with nums[i] >= target
        ans = -1 ;
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(check(mid , nums , target))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        if(ans != -1 && nums[ans] == target)
        {
            return ans ;
        }
        else
        {
            return -1 ;
        }
    }
};
34. Find First and Last Position of Element in Sorted Array Medium

LeetCode · cf/Find_First_and_Last_Position_of_Element_in_Sorted_Array.cpp

// orz
// 34. Find First and Last Position of Element in Sorted Array · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an array of integers nums sorted in non-decreasing order, find the starting
// and ending position of a given target value. if target is not found in the array,
// return [-1, -1]. you must write an algorithm with O(log n) runtime complexity.
// Example 1: nums = [5,7,7,8,8,10], target = 8 -> [3,4]
// Example 2: nums = [5,7,7,8,8,10], target = 6 -> [-1,-1]
// Example 3: nums = [], target = 0             -> [-1,-1]
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 10^5, -10^9 <= nums[i], target <= 10^9, nums is non-decreasing.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    first = min i with nums[i] = target, last = max i with nums[i] = target.
//    return [first, last], or [-1, -1] if no i has nums[i] = target.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    scan left to right, remember the first and the last index equal to target.
//    O(n), the judge asks for O(log n).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    nums is sorted, so all copies of target sit in one block, and both ends of
//    a block in a sorted array can be found by halving.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    both ends are "first index where check is true" questions:
//    first = the first i with nums[i] >= target,
//    last  = (the first i with nums[i] >= target + 1) - 1, i.e. one before the first value bigger than target.
//    (target + 1 is fine: values are integers, and it is done in ll so 10^9 + 1 can't overflow.)
//    one helper, firstatleast(x), with my binary search template, called twice.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : check(i , x) returns true if nums[i] >= x. false .. false true .. true because nums is sorted.
//    step 2 : firstatleast(x) = the binary search template, lo = 0, hi = n - 1, ans = n
//             (n means every value is < x).
//    step 3 : first = firstatleast(target). if first = n or nums[first] != target, target is
//             missing: return [-1, -1]. (n = 0 lands here too.)
//    step 4 : last = firstatleast(target + 1) - 1.
//    step 5 : return [first, last].
//
// 6. Why it works
//    in a sorted array the values equal to target are exactly the indices in
//    [first i with value >= target , first i with value > target). value > target is the same as
//    value >= target + 1 for integers. time O(log n) (two binary searches), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 1
bool check(ll i , vector<int>& nums , ll x)
{
    // this returns true if nums[i] >= x
    if(nums[i] >= x)
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
// step 2
ll firstatleast(vector<int>& nums , ll x)
{
    // this returns the first index i with nums[i] >= x, n if there is none
    ll n = nums.size() ;
    ll lo = 0 , hi = n - 1 , ans = n ;
    while(lo <= hi)
    {
        ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
        if(check(mid , nums , x))
        {
            ans = mid ;
            hi = mid - 1 ;
        }
        else
        {
            lo = mid + 1 ;
        }
    }
    return ans ;
}
class Solution {
public:
    vector<int> searchRange(vector<int>& nums, int target) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 3
        ll first = firstatleast(nums , target) ;
        if(first == n || nums[first] != target)
        {
            return {-1 , -1} ;
        }
        // step 4
        ll last = firstatleast(nums , (ll)target + 1) - 1 ;
        // step 5
        return {(int)first , (int)last} ;
    }
};
153. Find Minimum in Rotated Sorted Array Medium

LeetCode · cf/Find_Minimum_in_Rotated_Sorted_Array.cpp

// orz
// 153. Find Minimum in Rotated Sorted Array · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// an array of length n sorted in ascending order is rotated between 1 and n times.
// rotating [a0, a1, .., a(n-1)] once gives [a(n-1), a0, a1, .., a(n-2)].
// for example [0,1,2,4,5,6,7] can become [4,5,6,7,0,1,2] (rotated 4 times) or stay
// [0,1,2,4,5,6,7] (rotated 7 times). given the rotated array nums of unique
// elements, return its minimum element. you must write an O(log n) algorithm.
// Example 1: nums = [3,4,5,1,2]        -> 1
// Example 2: nums = [4,5,6,7,0,1,2]    -> 0
// Example 3: nums = [11,13,15,17]      -> 11
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 5000, -5000 <= nums[i] <= 5000, all values unique,
//              nums is sorted and rotated between 1 and n times.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    nums = (piece A)(piece B), both pieces ascending, every value of A bigger than every
//    value of B (A may be empty). return min over i of nums[i], which is the first element of B.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    scan all n values for the smallest. O(n), the judge asks for O(log n).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the array is two sorted pieces, and one comparison tells which piece mid is in.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    compare with the last element nums[n - 1], which always lives in piece B.
//    every value of B is <= nums[n - 1] (B is ascending and ends there),
//    every value of A is > nums[n - 1] (A is bigger than all of B).
//    so check(i) = "nums[i] <= nums[n - 1]" reads false on A, true on B: false .. false true .. true.
//    the minimum is the first index where check is true. my binary search template.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : check(i) returns true if nums[i] <= nums[n - 1], i.e. i is in piece B.
//    step 2 : lo = 0, hi = n - 1, ans = n - 1 (check(n - 1) is always true).
//    step 3 : the binary search template for the first index where check is true.
//    step 4 : return nums[ans].
//
// 6. Why it works
//    the first index of piece B is the start of the original sorted array, so it holds the minimum.
//    if the array was rotated n times, A is empty, check is true everywhere and ans = 0.
//    time O(log n), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 1
bool check(ll i , vector<int>& nums)
{
    // this returns true if index i is in piece B (nums[i] <= the last element)
    ll n = nums.size() ;
    if(nums[i] <= nums[n - 1])
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int findMin(vector<int>& nums) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 2
        ll lo = 0 , hi = n - 1 , ans = n - 1 ;
        // step 3 : first index of piece B
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(check(mid , nums))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return nums[ans] ;
    }
};
4. Median of Two Sorted Arrays Hard

LeetCode · cf/Median_of_Two_Sorted_Arrays.cpp

// orz
// 4. Median of Two Sorted Arrays · LeetCode · Hard
//
// Question:
// given two sorted arrays nums1 and nums2 of size m and n, return the median of the
// two sorted arrays together. the overall run time complexity should be O(log (m + n)).
// Example 1: nums1 = [1,3], nums2 = [2]    -> 2.00000   (merged [1,2,3], median 2)
// Example 2: nums1 = [1,2], nums2 = [3,4]  -> 2.50000   (merged [1,2,3,4], median (2 + 3) / 2)
// Constraints: 0 <= m, n <= 1000, 1 <= m + n <= 2000, -10^6 <= nums1[i], nums2[i] <= 10^6
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    let c = the merged sorted list of all m + n values, t = m + n.
//    t odd  -> answer c[(t - 1) / 2].
//    t even -> answer (c[t / 2 - 1] + c[t / 2]) / 2.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    merge the two arrays with two pointers and read the middle. O(m + n),
//    the judge asks for O(log(m + n)).
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it builds the whole merged list, but the median only needs to know where the
//    list is cut in half: which values go to the left half and which to the right.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    let half = (t + 1) / 2 = the size of the left half (one more than the right when t is odd).
//    the left half of c is always "the first i values of nums1 plus the first j values of nums2"
//    with i + j = half, because both arrays are sorted. so I only choose i, and j = half - i.
//    write a = nums1, b = nums2, and a[-1] = b[-1] = -INF, a[m] = b[n] = +INF for the borders.
//    a cut i is right when every left value <= every right value. inside a and inside b that is
//    free (sorted), so only the two cross pairs matter:
//        a[i - 1] <= b[j]    and    b[j - 1] <= a[i].
//    check(i) = "b[j - 1] <= a[i]" (with j = half - i).
//    when i grows by 1, a[i] can only grow and j shrinks so b[j - 1] can only shrink:
//    once check is true it stays true. false .. false true .. true. my binary search template.
//    the FIRST i with check true also has the other pair: check(i - 1) is false, so
//    b[j] > a[i - 1] (for i - 1 the j is j + 1, and b[(j + 1) - 1] = b[j]). and if i = 0, a[-1] = -INF.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : make nums1 the shorter array (call again with the two swapped), so m <= n.
//             then j = half - i stays inside [0 , n] for every i in [0 , m].
//    step 2 : at(x , k) returns x[k], or -INF for k < 0, or +INF for k >= size. check(i) as above.
//    step 3 : binary search template over i in [0 , m], ans = m at the start
//             (check(m) is true, a[m] = +INF).
//    step 4 : i = ans, j = half - i.
//             leftmax = max(a[i - 1] , b[j - 1]) = the biggest value of the left half,
//             rightmin = min(a[i] , b[j]) = the smallest value of the right half.
//    step 5 : t odd -> leftmax (the left half has the one extra value, the middle one).
//             t even -> (leftmax + rightmin) / 2.0.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    at the cut from step 3 both cross pairs hold, so every left value <= every right value,
//    so the left half is exactly c[0 .. half - 1]. then leftmax = c[half - 1] and rightmin = c[half],
//    which are the middle values. why m <= n keeps j legal: half - m >= 0 since half >= m,
//    and half - 0 <= n since (m + n + 1) / 2 <= (2n + 1) / 2 = n.
//    time O(log(min(m , n))) which is within O(log(m + n)), extra space O(1) (nothing is copied).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 2
ll at(vector<int>& x , ll k)
{
    // this returns x[k], with -INF before the start and +INF after the end
    ll sz = x.size() ;
    if(k < 0)
    {
        return -INF ;
    }
    else if(k >= sz)
    {
        return INF ;
    }
    else
    {
        return x[k] ;
    }
}
bool check(ll i , vector<int>& a , vector<int>& b , ll half)
{
    // this returns true if, taking i values from a and half - i from b,
    // the last value taken from b is <= the first value not taken from a
    ll j = half - i ;
    if(at(b , j - 1) <= at(a , i))
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    double findMedianSortedArrays(vector<int>& nums1, vector<int>& nums2) {
        ll m = nums1.size() ;
        ll n = nums2.size() ;
        // step 1 : binary search on the shorter array
        if(m > n)
        {
            return findMedianSortedArrays(nums2 , nums1) ;
        }
        ll t = m + n ;
        ll half = (t + 1) / 2 ;
        // step 3 : first i where check is true
        ll lo = 0 , hi = m , ans = m ;
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(check(mid , nums1 , nums2 , half))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        ll i = ans ;
        ll j = half - i ;
        ll leftmax = max(at(nums1 , i - 1) , at(nums2 , j - 1)) ;
        ll rightmin = min(at(nums1 , i) , at(nums2 , j)) ;
        // step 5
        if(t % 2 == 1)
        {
            return leftmax ;
        }
        else
        {
            return (leftmax + rightmin) / 2.0 ;
        }
    }
};

Heap

215. Kth Largest Element in an Array Medium

LeetCode · cf/Kth_Largest_Element_in_an_Array.cpp

// orz
// 215. Kth Largest Element in an Array · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an integer array nums and an integer k, return the kth largest element in
// the array. note that it is the kth largest element in sorted order, not the kth
// distinct element. can you solve it without sorting?
// Example 1: nums = [3,2,1,5,6,4], k = 2         -> 5
// Example 2: nums = [3,2,3,1,2,4,5,5,6], k = 4   -> 4
// Constraints: 1 <= k <= n <= 10^5, -10^4 <= nums[i] <= 10^4
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    sort nums in decreasing order as d[0] >= d[1] >= .. >= d[n - 1]. return d[k - 1].
//
// 2. Brute force
//    k times: find the biggest value left and remove it. the k-th one removed is the answer.
//    O(n * k), n = k = 10^5 gives 10^10. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    every round rescans all n values, but at any moment only the k biggest values seen
//    so far can still be the answer. everything below them is dead forever.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    keep exactly the k biggest values seen so far in a min-heap (priority_queue with greater).
//    the top of a min-heap is its smallest value, so the top = the k-th biggest seen so far.
//    a new value pushes in, and if the heap grows to k + 1, the top (the smallest of the k + 1)
//    can't be in the top k any more, so it is popped.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : min-heap pq, empty.
//    step 2 : for every x in nums: push x. if pq has k + 1 values, pop the top.
//    step 3 : return pq.top().
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after each x, pq holds the k biggest values among those seen (a value is popped only when
//    k bigger-or-equal values are in the heap with it). after all of nums, pq holds the k biggest
//    of the whole array, and its smallest one is the k-th largest.
//    time O(n log k), extra space O(k).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int findKthLargest(vector<int>& nums, int k) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 1 : this stores the k biggest values seen so far, smallest on top
        priority_queue < ll , vector < ll > , greater < ll > > pq ;
        // step 2
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            pq.push(nums[i]) ;
            if((ll)pq.size() > k)
            {
                pq.pop() ;
            }
        }
        // step 3
        return pq.top() ;
    }
};
502. IPO Hard

LeetCode · cf/IPO.cpp

// orz
// 502. IPO · LeetCode · Hard
//
// Question:
// a company wants to finish at most k distinct projects before its IPO. there are n
// projects; project i has a pure profit profits[i] and needs at least capital[i]
// capital to start. you start with w capital. finishing a project adds its pure
// profit to your capital. pick at most k distinct projects to maximise your final
// capital, and return that final maximised capital. the answer fits in a 32-bit
// signed integer.
// Example 1: k = 2, w = 0, profits = [1,2,3], capital = [0,1,1] -> 4
//            (start project 0 with w = 0, now w = 1, then project 2, w = 1 + 3 = 4)
// Example 2: k = 3, w = 0, profits = [1,2,3], capital = [0,1,2] -> 6
// Constraints: 1 <= k <= 10^5, 0 <= w <= 10^9, 1 <= n <= 10^5,
//              0 <= profits[i] <= 10^4, 0 <= capital[i] <= 10^9
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    choose an order p1, p2, .., pr of distinct projects, r <= k, such that
//    capital[p(s)] <= w + profits[p1] + .. + profits[p(s - 1)] for every s.
//    maximise w + profits[p1] + .. + profits[pr].
//
// 2. Brute force
//    every round, scan all projects, take the most profitable one I can afford and haven't done.
//    O(n * k) = 10^10. dead. (trying all orders is even worse.)
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    profits are >= 0, so w never goes down. a project I can afford now stays affordable
//    forever, yet every round re-checks all of them from scratch.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    greedy: each round take the most profitable affordable project (exchange argument in 6.).
//    sort projects by capital. as w grows, walk a pointer p and push every newly affordable
//    project's profit into a max-heap, each project exactly once. the top of the heap is the
//    best affordable project. sorted list + pointer + heap.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : v = pairs (capital[i] , profits[i]), sorted by capital.
//    step 2 : pq = max-heap of profits of projects that are affordable and not done. p = 0.
//    step 3 : repeat k times:
//             while p < n and v[p].capital <= w : push v[p].profit, p++.
//    step 4 :     if pq is empty, nothing is affordable and w can't grow any more: stop.
//                 else w += pq.top(), pop it.
//    step 5 : return w.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    exchange argument: say an optimal plan does some project x first, and g is the most
//    profitable affordable one. if the plan uses g later, swap x and g: g first is affordable,
//    and after it w is at least as big as after x, so everything later stays affordable.
//    if the plan never uses g, do g instead of x: same reason, and the final w is not smaller.
//    so taking g first is never worse; repeat the argument for the rest of the rounds.
//    time O(n log n + k log n), extra space O(n).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int findMaximizedCapital(int k, int w, vector<int>& profits, vector<int>& capital) {
        ll n = profits.size() ;
        ll cur = w ;
        // step 1 : (capital , profit), sorted by capital
        vector < pll > v(n) ;
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            v[i].fr = capital[i] ;
            v[i].sc = profits[i] ;
        }
        sort(v.begin() , v.end() , [](pll a , pll b)
        {
            return a.fr < b.fr ;
        }) ;
        // step 2 : this stores the profits of the affordable projects not done yet, biggest on top
        priority_queue < ll > pq ;
        ll p = 0 ;
        for(ll round = 0 ; round < k ; round++)
        {
            // step 3 : push every project that just became affordable
            while(p < n && v[p].fr <= cur)
            {
                pq.push(v[p].sc) ;
                p++ ;
            }
            // step 4
            if(pq.empty())
            {
                break ;
            }
            else
            {
                cur += pq.top() ;
                pq.pop() ;
            }
        }
        // step 5
        return cur ;
    }
};
373. Find K Pairs with Smallest Sums Medium

LeetCode · cf/Find_K_Pairs_with_Smallest_Sums.cpp

// orz
// 373. Find K Pairs with Smallest Sums · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// you are given two integer arrays nums1 and nums2 sorted in non-decreasing order,
// and an integer k. a pair (u, v) has one element u from nums1 and one element v
// from nums2. return the k pairs (u1, v1), (u2, v2), .., (uk, vk) with the smallest sums.
// Example 1: nums1 = [1,7,11], nums2 = [2,4,6], k = 3 -> [[1,2],[1,4],[1,6]]
// Example 2: nums1 = [1,1,2], nums2 = [1,2,3], k = 2  -> [[1,1],[1,1]]
// Constraints: 1 <= n1, n2 <= 10^5, -10^9 <= nums1[i], nums2[i] <= 10^9,
//              both sorted in non-decreasing order, 1 <= k <= 10^4, k <= n1 * n2
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    among all index pairs (i , j), 0 <= i < n1, 0 <= j < n2, output k of them with the
//    smallest nums1[i] + nums2[j] (as values [nums1[i] , nums2[j]]).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    build all n1 * n2 pairs, sort by sum, take the first k.
//    n1 * n2 = 10^10. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    only k <= 10^4 pairs are needed, and it builds and sorts all 10^10.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    fix i. row i = the pairs (i , 0), (i , 1), (i , 2), .. and because nums2 is sorted,
//    their sums only go up along the row. so I have n1 sorted rows and I want the k smallest
//    values over all rows: a k-way merge with a min-heap, like Dijkstra's pq.
//    the heap holds the current front of every row. the smallest pair not yet taken is always
//    some row's front, so pop the top, output it, and push the next pair of that row.
//    only rows i < k matter: row i >= k starts at (i , 0), and (0 , 0) .. (k - 1 , 0) are
//    k pairs with sums <= it (nums1 sorted), so it is never needed.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : min-heap pq of {sum , {i , j}}.
//    step 2 : push the front of each row: {nums1[i] + nums2[0] , {i , 0}} for i < min(n1 , k).
//    step 3 : k times: pop the top {sum , {i , j}}, add [nums1[i] , nums2[j]] to ans.
//             if j + 1 < n2, push the next pair of row i: {nums1[i] + nums2[j + 1] , {i , j + 1}}.
//    step 4 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every pair not yet output is at or behind its row's front, and its row is sorted, so it is
//    >= that front. so the smallest pair left is the smallest front = pq.top().
//    sums reach 2 * 10^9, so they are kept in ll.
//    time O(k log k) (the heap never has more than k entries), extra space O(k).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    vector<vector<int>> kSmallestPairs(vector<int>& nums1, vector<int>& nums2, int k) {
        ll n1 = nums1.size() ;
        ll n2 = nums2.size() ;
        vector < vector < int > > ans ;
        // step 1 : this stores {sum , {i , j}} for the front of every row, smallest sum on top
        priority_queue < pair < ll , pll > , vector < pair < ll , pll > > , greater < pair < ll , pll > > > pq ;
        // step 2 : the front of rows 0 .. min(n1 , k) - 1
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < min(n1 , (ll)k) ; i++)
        {
            ll sum = (ll)nums1[i] + nums2[0] ;
            pq.push({sum , {i , 0}}) ;
        }
        // step 3
        for(ll cnt = 0 ; cnt < k ; cnt++)
        {
            pair < ll , pll > top = pq.top() ;
            pq.pop() ;
            ll i = top.sc.fr ;
            ll j = top.sc.sc ;
            ans.push_back({nums1[i] , nums2[j]}) ;
            if(j + 1 < n2)
            {
                ll sum = (ll)nums1[i] + nums2[j + 1] ;
                pq.push({sum , {i , j + 1}}) ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};
295. Find Median from Data Stream Hard

LeetCode · cf/Find_Median_from_Data_Stream.cpp

// orz
// 295. Find Median from Data Stream · LeetCode · Hard
//
// Question:
// the median is the middle value in an ordered integer list. if the size of the list
// is even, there is no single middle value, and the median is the mean of the two
// middle values. (for [2,3,4] the median is 3, for [2,3] it is (2 + 3) / 2 = 2.5.)
// implement the MedianFinder class:
//   MedianFinder() initialises the object.
//   void addNum(int num) adds the integer num from the data stream.
//   double findMedian() returns the median of all elements so far.
//   answers within 10^-5 of the actual answer are accepted.
// Example 1: ["MedianFinder","addNum","addNum","findMedian","addNum","findMedian"]
//            [[],[1],[2],[],[3],[]]  -> [null,null,null,1.5,null,2.0]
// Constraints: -10^5 <= num <= 10^5, findMedian is called only when there is at least
//              one element, at most 5 * 10^4 calls to addNum and findMedian in total.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    keep a multiset S under insertions. with c = S sorted and t = |S|, answer
//    c[(t - 1) / 2] when t is odd, (c[t / 2 - 1] + c[t / 2]) / 2 when t is even.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    keep a sorted vector, insert each number at its place (shift everything after it).
//    O(t) per add, 5 * 10^4 adds -> about 1.25 * 10^9 shifts. too slow.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it keeps the whole list in order, but the median only needs the two values at the cut
//    in the middle: the biggest of the smaller half and the smallest of the bigger half.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    split the numbers into two halves:
//    lo = the smaller half in a max-heap (its top = the biggest small value),
//    hi = the bigger half in a min-heap  (its top = the smallest big value).
//    keep two rules: every value in lo <= every value in hi, and
//    lo.size() = hi.size() or lo.size() = hi.size() + 1.
//    then the median is read from the two tops.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : members lo (max-heap) and hi (min-heap), both empty.
//    step 2 : addNum: push num into lo.
//    step 3 :         move lo's top into hi. lo's top is lo's biggest value, so after the move
//                     every value in lo is still <= every value in hi.
//    step 4 :         if hi now has more values than lo, move hi's top (its smallest) back into lo.
//                     this fixes the sizes and keeps the order rule.
//    step 5 : findMedian: lo bigger -> t is odd and the middle value is lo.top().
//             same size -> (lo.top() + hi.top()) / 2.0.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after step 3: num went into the halves, and the order rule holds because only lo's biggest moved up.
//    sizes before addNum: (s , s) or (s + 1 , s). after step 2 and 3 they are (s , s + 1) or (s + 1 , s + 1),
//    step 4 turns (s , s + 1) into (s + 1 , s). so both rules hold after every addNum.
//    with the rules, lo is exactly the first ceil(t / 2) values of the sorted list, so its top and
//    hi's top are the middle values. time O(log t) per addNum, O(1) per findMedian, space O(t).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class MedianFinder {
public:
    // step 1
    priority_queue < ll > lo ;                                  // this stores the smaller half, biggest on top
    priority_queue < ll , vector < ll > , greater < ll > > hi ; // this stores the bigger half, smallest on top
    MedianFinder() {
    }

    void addNum(int num) {
        // step 2
        lo.push(num) ;
        // step 3
        hi.push(lo.top()) ;
        lo.pop() ;
        // step 4
        if(hi.size() > lo.size())
        {
            lo.push(hi.top()) ;
            hi.pop() ;
        }
    }

    double findMedian() {
        // step 5
        if(lo.size() > hi.size())
        {
            return lo.top() ;
        }
        else
        {
            return (lo.top() + hi.top()) / 2.0 ;
        }
    }
};

Bit Manipulation

67. Add Binary Easy

LeetCode · cf/Add_Binary.cpp

// orz
// 67. Add Binary · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given two binary strings a and b, return their sum as a binary string.
// Example 1: a = "11", b = "1"        -> "100"
// Example 2: a = "1010", b = "1011"   -> "10101"
// Constraints: 1 <= a.length, b.length <= 10^4, a and b contain only '0' and '1',
//              no leading zeros except the number zero itself.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    value(s) = sum over k of s[k] * 2^(len - 1 - k) (the last char is the 2^0 digit).
//    return the binary string (no leading zeros) of value(a) + value(b).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    turn a and b into integers, add, turn the sum back into binary.
//    dies at once: 10^4 bits is a number near 2^10000, no integer type holds it.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it needs the whole number at once, but school addition only ever needs one column
//    and the carry coming into it.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    simulate the statement literally: column addition in base 2, from the last char.
//    in one column: s = carry + (bit of a) + (bit of b), so s is 0, 1, 2 or 3.
//    the digit written is s % 2 and the carry to the next column is s / 2.
//    (s = 2 is "10" in binary: write 0, carry 1. s = 3 is "11": write 1, carry 1.)
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : i = last index of a, j = last index of b, carry = 0, ans = "".
//    step 2 : while i >= 0 or j >= 0 or carry = 1:
//             s = carry, plus a[i] - '0' if i >= 0, plus b[j] - '0' if j >= 0.
//             append the char '0' + s % 2, carry = s / 2, i--, j--.
//    step 3 : the digits were appended lowest first, so reverse ans and return it.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    it is the column-by-column addition we do by hand, in base 2. the loop runs while a column
//    still has a digit or a carry, so the last carry becomes the leading 1 (like "11" + "1" = "100").
//    no leading zeros: a column runs only when it has a digit or a carry, and the top column ends in a
//    1 unless both inputs are "0". time O(max(|a| , |b|)), extra space O(1) besides the answer.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    string addBinary(string a, string b) {
        // step 1
        ll i = (ll)a.size() - 1 ;
        ll j = (ll)b.size() - 1 ;
        ll carry = 0 ;
        string ans = "" ;
        // step 2 : one column per round, from the right
        while(i >= 0 || j >= 0 || carry == 1)
        {
            ll s = carry ;
            if(i >= 0)
            {
                s += a[i] - '0' ;
            }
            if(j >= 0)
            {
                s += b[j] - '0' ;
            }
            ans += (char)('0' + s % 2) ;
            carry = s / 2 ;
            i-- ;
            j-- ;
        }
        // step 3
        reverse(ans.begin() , ans.end()) ;
        return ans ;
    }
};
190. Reverse Bits Easy

LeetCode · cf/Reverse_Bits.cpp

// orz
// 190. Reverse Bits · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// reverse the bits of a given 32 bit signed integer: write n with exactly 32 bits
// (leading zeros included), read those 32 bits backwards, and return that number.
// Example 1: n = 43261596   -> 964176192
//            00000010100101000001111010011100 -> 00111001011110000010100101000000
// Example 2: n = 2147483644 -> 1073741822
//            01111111111111111111111111111100 -> 00111111111111111111111111111110
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 2^31 - 2, n is even.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    n = sum over b = 0 .. 31 of bit(b) * 2^b. return sum over b of bit(b) * 2^(31 - b):
//    bit b of n becomes bit 31 - b of the answer.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    write n as a 32 char string of '0' / '1' (repeated % 2 and / 2), reverse the string,
//    read it back as a number. 32 steps, fine, but it builds a string just to move bits.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the bits are already inside n. they can be read and written in place, no string needed.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    two small bit tools, nothing else:
//    read bit b of x  : (x >> b) & 1.
//        ">> b" slides every bit of x b places to the right, so bit b lands in place 0.
//        "& 1" keeps only place 0 (1 is 000..001, so every other place is ANDed with 0).
//        the result is 0 or 1: the value of bit b.
//    turn on bit p of ans : ans = ans | (1LL << p).
//        "1LL << p" is a number with only bit p on (it is 2^p).
//        "|" puts a 1 in every place where either side has a 1, so bit p of ans becomes 1
//        and every other bit of ans stays as it was.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : x = n as ll (n >= 0, so there is no sign to worry about), ans = 0.
//    step 2 : for b = 0 .. 31: bit = (x >> b) & 1. if bit = 1, turn on bit 31 - b of ans.
//    step 3 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every bit of n is copied to its mirror place, and the places that get no 1 stay 0.
//    the answer fits in int: n is even, so bit 0 of n is 0, so bit 31 of the answer is 0,
//    so the answer is < 2^31. time O(32), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int reverseBits(int n) {
        // step 1
        ll x = n ;
        ll ans = 0 ;
        // step 2 : bit b of n goes to place 31 - b
        for(ll b = 0 ; b < 32 ; b++)
        {
            ll bit = (x >> b) & 1 ;
            if(bit == 1)
            {
                ans = ans | (1LL << (31 - b)) ;
            }
            else
            {
                continue ;
            }
        }
        // step 3
        return ans ;
    }
};
191. Number of 1 Bits Easy

LeetCode · cf/Number_of_1_Bits.cpp

// orz
// 191. Number of 1 Bits · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given a positive integer n, return the number of set bits (bits equal to 1)
// in its binary representation (also known as the Hamming weight).
// Example 1: n = 11          -> 3    (1011)
// Example 2: n = 128         -> 1    (10000000)
// Example 3: n = 2147483645  -> 30   (1111111111111111111111111111101)
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 2^31 - 1
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    n = sum over b = 0 .. 30 of bit(b) * 2^b with each bit(b) in {0 , 1}. return sum of bit(b).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    write n in binary as a string (repeated % 2 and / 2) and count the '1' chars.
//    31 steps, fine, but it builds a string it doesn't need.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the bits are already inside n, so each one can be read directly and added up.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    read bit b of x with (x >> b) & 1:
//        ">> b" slides every bit of x b places to the right, so bit b lands in place 0.
//        "& 1" keeps only place 0 (1 is 000..001, every other place is ANDed with 0).
//        the result is 0 or 1: the value of bit b. adding these up counts the ones.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : x = n as ll, cnt = 0.
//    step 2 : for b = 0 .. 31: cnt += (x >> b) & 1.
//    step 3 : return cnt.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    every bit place of n is visited once and adds exactly its own value, 0 or 1.
//    n < 2^31, so places 0 .. 31 cover every bit. time O(32), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int hammingWeight(int n) {
        // step 1
        ll x = n ;
        ll cnt = 0 ;
        // step 2 : add the value of every bit
        for(ll b = 0 ; b < 32 ; b++)
        {
            ll bit = (x >> b) & 1 ;
            cnt += bit ;
        }
        // step 3
        return cnt ;
    }
};
136. Single Number Easy

LeetCode · cf/Single_Number.cpp

// orz
// 136. Single Number · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given a non-empty array of integers nums, every element appears twice except
// for one. find that single one. you must implement a solution with linear
// runtime complexity and use only constant extra space.
// Example 1: nums = [2,2,1]       -> 1
// Example 2: nums = [4,1,2,1,2]   -> 4
// Example 3: nums = [1]           -> 1
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 3 * 10^4, -3 * 10^4 <= nums[i] <= 3 * 10^4,
//              every element appears twice except one element which appears once.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    the multiset nums = {u} plus two copies of each of some values. return u.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every i count how many j have nums[j] = nums[i], return the one with count 1.
//    O(n^2) = 9 * 10^8. too slow. (a count map is O(n) but O(n) extra space, the judge wants O(1).)
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it computes full counts, but only "odd or even" matters: u is the one value with an odd count.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    xor, ^, keeps exactly that odd / even information, in small steps:
//    (a) a ^ b looks at each bit place on its own: the result bit is 1 when the two bits differ,
//        0 when they are the same. so per bit place it is "add, and keep only odd / even" (1 + 1 = 0).
//    (b) x ^ x = 0, because every bit equals itself.
//    (c) x ^ 0 = x, because each bit of x differs from 0 exactly when it is 1.
//    (d) order and grouping don't matter: a ^ b = b ^ a and (a ^ b) ^ c = a ^ (b ^ c),
//        because per bit place it is just counting ones and keeping odd / even.
//    so xor of all of nums can be regrouped as u ^ (v1 ^ v1) ^ (v2 ^ v2) ^ .. = u ^ 0 ^ 0 .. = u.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : acc = 0.
//    step 2 : for every x in nums, acc = acc ^ x.
//    step 3 : return acc.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    by (d) the order of the array doesn't matter, by (b) the two copies of every pair cancel,
//    by (c) what is left is u. negative numbers are fine: xor only looks at bits.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int singleNumber(vector<int>& nums) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        // step 1
        ll acc = 0 ;
        // step 2 : pairs cancel, the single one stays
        for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
        {
            acc = acc ^ nums[i] ;
        }
        // step 3
        return acc ;
    }
};
137. Single Number II Medium

LeetCode · cf/Single_Number_II.cpp

// orz
// 137. Single Number II · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an integer array nums where every element appears exactly three times except
// for one, which appears exactly once. find the single element and return it.
// you must implement a solution with linear runtime complexity and use only
// constant extra space.
// Example 1: nums = [2,2,3,2]             -> 3
// Example 2: nums = [0,1,0,1,0,1,99]      -> 99
// Constraints: 1 <= n <= 3 * 10^4, -2^31 <= nums[i] <= 2^31 - 1,
//              every element appears exactly three times except one which appears once.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    the multiset nums = {u} plus three copies of each of some values. return u.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every i count how many j have nums[j] = nums[i], return the one with count 1.
//    O(n^2) = 9 * 10^8. too slow. (a count map is O(n) but O(n) extra space, the judge wants O(1).)
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    it counts whole values. xor from Single Number doesn't help (three copies xor to the value,
//    not to 0), but the same idea of looking at one bit place at a time does.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    look at one bit place b at a time and count how many numbers have bit b on.
//    a value that appears three times adds 0 or 3 to that count, u adds 1 or 0.
//    so count % 3 = bit b of u. 32 places, each one a simple count.
//    the bit tools, in small steps:
//    read bit b of x : (x >> b) & 1. ">> b" slides bit b into place 0, "& 1" keeps only place 0.
//    negative numbers: an int is 32 bits in two's complement, and bit 31 is the sign bit,
//    worth -2^31 instead of +2^31 (e.g. -1 = all 32 bits on = -2^31 + (2^31 - 1)).
//    putting the int in an ll copies bit 31 into all the new high bits, so bits 0 .. 31 read the same.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : for b = 0 .. 31: cnt = number of x in nums with (x >> b) & 1 = 1.
//    step 2 :     if cnt % 3 = 1, bit b of u is on.
//    step 3 :     add what that bit is worth: 2^b for b = 0 .. 30, and -2^31 for the sign bit b = 31.
//    step 4 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    per bit place the triples add a multiple of 3, so the remainder mod 3 is exactly u's bit.
//    rebuilding with the two's complement weights gives u itself, negative or not.
//    time O(32 * n), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int singleNumber(vector<int>& nums) {
        ll n = nums.size() ;
        ll ans = 0 ;
        for(ll b = 0 ; b < 32 ; b++)
        {
            // step 1 : how many numbers have bit b on
            ll cnt = 0 ;
            for(ll i = 0 ; i < n ; i++)
            {
                ll x = nums[i] ;
                cnt += (x >> b) & 1 ;
            }
            // step 2 : cnt % 3 is 0 or 1, and 0 means bit b of u is off
            if(cnt % 3 == 0)
            {
                continue ;
            }
            // step 3 : bit b of u is on
            if(b < 31)
            {
                ans += (1LL << b) ;
            }
            else
            {
                ans -= (1LL << 31) ; // the sign bit
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};
201. Bitwise AND of Numbers Range Medium

LeetCode · cf/Bitwise_AND_of_Numbers_Range.cpp

// orz
// 201. Bitwise AND of Numbers Range · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given two integers left and right that represent the range [left, right], return
// the bitwise AND of all numbers in this range, inclusive.
// Example 1: left = 5, right = 7              -> 4   (101 & 110 & 111 = 100)
// Example 2: left = 0, right = 0              -> 0
// Example 3: left = 1, right = 2147483647     -> 0
// Constraints: 0 <= left <= right <= 2^31 - 1
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    return left & (left + 1) & .. & right.
//    "&" looks at each bit place on its own: the result bit is 1 only if every number has a 1 there.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    AND every number from left to right. up to 2^31 numbers. dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    a bit place becomes 0 as soon as one number in the range has a 0 there,
//    and in a long range the low bits flip all the time. most of the work is on bits already dead.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    write left and right in binary. they agree on some top bits (the common prefix), then at the
//    first place p from the top where they differ, left has 0 and right has 1 (because left < right).
//    (a) the prefix survives: every x in [left , right] has the same top bits, since x sits between
//        two numbers that both start with that prefix.
//    (b) every place <= p dies: m = prefix, then 1 at p, then all 0 is <= right (right has 1 at p),
//        and m - 1 = prefix, then 0 at p, then all 1 is >= left (left has 0 at p). so both m - 1 and m
//        are in the range, and at every place <= p one of them has a 0.
//    so the answer = the common prefix, followed by zeros.
//    bit tools: x >> 1 drops the lowest bit of x (everything slides one place right).
//               x << s adds s zeros at the bottom (everything slides s places left).
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : shift = 0.
//    step 2 : while left != right: drop the lowest bit of both (>> 1), shift++.
//             when they become equal, only the common prefix is left.
//    step 3 : return left << shift: the prefix back in its place, zeros under it.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    step 2 stops exactly when everything below the common prefix has been dropped, and (a) + (b)
//    say the answer is that prefix with zeros under it. left = right gives left itself.
//    time O(31), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int rangeBitwiseAnd(int left, int right) {
        ll l = left ;
        ll r = right ;
        // step 1
        ll shift = 0 ;
        // step 2 : drop bits until only the common prefix is left
        while(l != r)
        {
            l = l >> 1 ;
            r = r >> 1 ;
            shift++ ;
        }
        // step 3 : the prefix with zeros under it
        return l << shift ;
    }
};

Math

9. Palindrome Number Easy

LeetCode · cf/Palindrome_Number.cpp

// orz
// 9. Palindrome Number · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given an integer x, return true if x is a palindrome (it reads the same
// from left to right and from right to left), and false otherwise.
// Example 1: x = 121  -> true
// Example 2: x = -121 -> false  (right to left it reads 121-)
// Example 3: x = 10   -> false  (right to left it reads 01)
// Constraints: -2^31 <= x <= 2^31 - 1
// Follow up: solve it without converting the integer to a string. -> done here.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    write x = d(k - 1) d(k - 2) .. d(1) d(0) in base 10.
//    x is a palindrome iff x >= 0 and d(i) = d(k - 1 - i) for every i,
//    i.e. x equals the number whose digits are x's digits reversed.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    turn x into a string, reverse it, compare.
//    O(digits) time, but it builds a string, which the follow up forbids.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    the string is only a way to read the digits back to front.
//    % 10 and / 10 already hand me the digits back to front, one by one.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    build rev = x with its digits reversed, using only % 10 and / 10.
//    x is a palindrome iff rev == x. rev can pass 2^31 - 1 (2147483647 reversed
//    is 7463847412), so rev is ll.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : x < 0 -> false. the '-' sits on the left only.
//    step 2 : temp = x, rev = 0. while temp > 0 : digit = temp % 10,
//             rev = rev * 10 + digit, temp /= 10.
//             every round moves the last digit of temp to the end of rev.
//    step 3 : return rev == x.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    after step 2 rev has the digits d(0) d(1) .. d(k - 1), x reversed.
//    two numbers without leading zeros are equal iff their digit strings are equal,
//    so rev == x iff x reads the same both ways. (x = 10 gives rev = 1 != 10, correct.)
//    time O(number of digits) = O(log x), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    bool isPalindrome(int x) {
        // step 1 : a negative number is never a palindrome
        if(x < 0)
        {
            return false ;
        }
        // step 2 : rev = x with its digits reversed
        ll temp = x ;
        ll rev = 0 ;
        while(temp > 0)
        {
            ll digit = temp % 10 ;
            rev = rev * 10 + digit ;
            temp /= 10 ;
        }
        // step 3
        if(rev == x)
        {
            return true ;
        }
        else
        {
            return false ;
        }
    }
};
66. Plus One Easy

LeetCode · cf/Plus_One.cpp

// orz
// 66. Plus One · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// a large integer is given as an array digits, where digits[i] is the i-th digit,
// most significant first. there are no leading zeros. add one to the integer and
// return the resulting array of digits.
// Example 1: digits = [1,2,3]   -> [1,2,4]
// Example 2: digits = [4,3,2,1] -> [4,3,2,2]
// Example 3: digits = [9]       -> [1,0]
// Constraints: 1 <= digits.length <= 100, 0 <= digits[i] <= 9,
//              digits has no leading 0's
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    N = sum of digits[i] * 10^(n - 1 - i). return the base 10 digits of N + 1.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    turn digits into a number, add 1, split it back into digits.
//    100 digits do not fit in any integer type (ll stops at 19 digits). dead.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    adding 1 only touches the last digit and the carry it makes.
//    the number itself never has to exist.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    do school addition on the array: add 1 to the last digit and push the carry left.
//    the carry is 0 or 1, so everything stays a single digit.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : carry = 1 (this is the "+ 1").
//    step 2 : walk i from n - 1 down to 0 : sum = digits[i] + carry,
//             digits[i] = sum % 10, carry = sum / 10.
//    step 3 : if carry is still 1, every digit was 9 and is now 0, so the
//             answer is one digit longer: put a 1 in front.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    this is exactly the column addition of N + 1, right to left.
//    a carry survives step 2 only when N = 99..9, and then N + 1 = 100..0, which is step 3.
//    time O(n), extra space O(1) apart from the answer.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    vector<int> plusOne(vector<int>& digits) {
        ll n = digits.size() ;
        vector < int > ans = digits ;
        // step 1 : the + 1 enters as a carry
        ll carry = 1 ;
        // step 2 : column addition from the last digit
        for(ll i = n - 1 ; i >= 0 ; i--)
        {
            ll sum = ans[i] + carry ;
            ans[i] = sum % 10 ;
            carry = sum / 10 ;
        }
        // step 3 : all nines, one more digit in front
        if(carry == 1)
        {
            ans.insert(ans.begin() , 1) ;
        }
        return ans ;
    }
};
172. Factorial Trailing Zeroes Medium

LeetCode · cf/Factorial_Trailing_Zeroes.cpp

// orz
// 172. Factorial Trailing Zeroes · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// given an integer n, return the number of trailing zeroes in n!.
// (n! = n * (n - 1) * .. * 3 * 2 * 1)
// Example 1: n = 3 -> 0  (3! = 6)
// Example 2: n = 5 -> 1  (5! = 120)
// Example 3: n = 0 -> 0  (0! = 1)
// Constraints: 0 <= n <= 10^4
// Follow up: a solution in logarithmic time complexity. -> done here.
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    the largest t with 10^t dividing n!.
//    10 = 2 * 5, so t = min(e2 , e5), where e2 / e5 = the power of 2 / 5 in n!.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    compute n! and count the zeros at the end.
//    10^4! has about 35660 digits, no integer type holds it. dead.
//    (better brute : for every k in 1 .. n, count how many 5s divide k. O(n log n), not log.)
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    only the factors 2 and 5 matter, and there are always more 2s than 5s
//    (every second number gives a 2, only every fifth gives a 5).
//    so t = e5. counting 5s number by number repeats the same division again and again.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    count 5s by layers instead of by numbers (Legendre):
//    floor(n / 5) numbers give at least one 5, floor(n / 25) give a second one,
//    floor(n / 125) a third, ... e5 = n / 5 + n / 25 + n / 125 + ..
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : ans = 0.
//    step 2 : for i = 5, 25, 125, .. while i <= n : ans += n / i.
//    step 3 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    a number k with exactly c factors 5 is a multiple of 5, 25, .., 5^c and of no
//    higher power, so the layers count it exactly c times. summing over k gives e5,
//    and e5 <= e2, so e5 = min(e2 , e5) = the number of trailing zeros.
//    time O(log5 n), extra space O(1). i is ll so i *= 5 can never overflow.
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    int trailingZeroes(int n) {
        // step 1
        ll ans = 0 ;
        // step 2 : the numbers that give a 5, then a second 5, then a third ..
        for(ll i = 5 ; i <= n ; i *= 5)
        {
            ans += n / i ;
        }
        // step 3
        return ans ;
    }
};
69. Sqrt(x) Easy

LeetCode · cf/Sqrt_x.cpp

// orz
// 69. Sqrt(x) · LeetCode · Easy
//
// Question:
// given a non-negative integer x, return the square root of x rounded down to the
// nearest integer. the returned integer should be non-negative as well.
// you must not use any built-in exponent function or operator (no pow(x , 0.5), no sqrt).
// Example 1: x = 4 -> 2
// Example 2: x = 8 -> 2  (sqrt(8) = 2.828.., rounded down)
// Constraints: 0 <= x <= 2^31 - 1
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    find the largest integer r >= 0 with r * r <= x.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    r = 0, 1, 2, .. while (r + 1) * (r + 1) <= x.
//    sqrt(2^31) is about 46341 steps. it passes, but it is a linear scan of a
//    sorted yes / no line, which binary search does in 32 steps.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    "r * r <= x" is true for r = 0 .. ans and false after it.
//    one check at mid tells me which side the answer is on, so I can throw half away.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    this is my binary search on the answer: a monotone bool check(mid),
//    and I want the last mid where it is true.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : check(mid , x) returns true if mid * mid <= x. mid and mid * mid are ll,
//             mid <= 2^31 - 1 so mid * mid < 4.7 * 10^18 fits.
//    step 2 : lo = 0, hi = x, ans = 0 (r = 0 always works).
//    step 3 : while lo <= hi : mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2.
//             check(mid) true  -> ans = mid, the answer is mid or bigger, lo = mid + 1.
//             check(mid) false -> the answer is smaller, hi = mid - 1.
//    step 4 : return ans.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    check is true on 0 .. r and false after r, and the answer r lies in [0 , x]
//    (x * x >= x for x >= 1, and r = 0 for x = 0). every true mid is stored in ans
//    before lo jumps past it, and the loop only drops halves that cannot hold a larger
//    true value, so ans ends at r.
//    time O(log x), extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
// step 1
bool check(ll mid , ll x)
{
    // this returns true if mid * mid <= x
    if(mid * mid <= x)
    {
        return true ;
    }
    else
    {
        return false ;
    }
}
class Solution {
public:
    int mySqrt(int x) {
        // step 2
        ll lo = 0 , hi = x , ans = 0 ;
        // step 3 : last mid where check is true
        while(lo <= hi)
        {
            ll mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 ;
            if(check(mid , x))
            {
                ans = mid ;
                lo = mid + 1 ;
            }
            else
            {
                hi = mid - 1 ;
            }
        }
        // step 4
        return ans ;
    }
};
50. Pow(x, n) Medium

LeetCode · cf/Pow_x_n.cpp

// orz
// 50. Pow(x, n) · LeetCode · Medium
//
// Question:
// implement pow(x , n), which calculates x raised to the power n (x^n).
// Example 1: x = 2.00000, n = 10 -> 1024.00000
// Example 2: x = 2.10000, n = 3  -> 9.26100
// Example 3: x = 2.00000, n = -2 -> 0.25000  (2^-2 = 1 / 4)
// Constraints: -100.0 < x < 100.0, -2^31 <= n <= 2^31 - 1, n is an integer,
//              either x is not zero or n > 0, -10^4 <= x^n <= 10^4
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    n >= 0 : x^n = x * x * .. * x (n times).
//    n < 0  : x^n = (1 / x)^(-n). x != 0 here (given), so 1 / x exists.
//
// 2. Brute force
//    multiply x into the answer |n| times.
//    |n| up to 2^31, about 2 * 10^9 multiplications per call. TLE.
//
// 3. What the brute force wastes
//    x^8 = x * x * x * x * x * x * x * x is 7 multiplications, but
//    x^2 = x * x, x^4 = x^2 * x^2, x^8 = x^4 * x^4 is 3. squares reuse the work.
//
// 4. Key breaking point
//    my binary exponentiation (mpow), with double instead of mod:
//    write b in binary, b = sum of 2^j over the set bits j, so
//    x^b = product of x^(2^j) over the set bits. x^(2^j) is x squared j times.
//    the trap : n = -2^31 and -n = 2^31 does not fit in int. so b is ll.
//
// 5. Method
//    step 1 : b = n as ll, a = x.
//             if b < 0 : a = 1 / a, b = -b. now b >= 0 and the answer is a^b.
//    step 2 : res = 1. while b > 0 :
//             if the last bit of b is 1 : res = res * a.
//             a = a * a (a goes x, x^2, x^4, ..), b >>= 1 (next bit).
//    step 3 : return res.
//
// 6. Why it works
//    at the start of round j, a = x^(2^j) and b holds the bits j, j + 1, .. of |n|.
//    res picks up a exactly when bit j is 1, so at the end res = product of x^(2^j)
//    over the set bits = x^|n|. the last a = a * a may overflow to inf or go to 0,
//    but that value is never multiplied into res.
//    time O(log |n|) = 32 rounds, extra space O(1).
#include <bits/stdc++.h>
#define ll long long
#define endl "\n"
#define fr first
#define sc second
#define MOD 1000000007
#define MOD1 998244353
#define INF 1e18
#define mp make_pair
#define PI acos(-1)
#define py cout << "YES" << endl
#define pn cout << "NO" << endl
#define pll pair<ll,ll>
using namespace std;
class Solution {
public:
    double myPow(double x, int n) {
        // step 1 : ll so that -(-2^31) fits
        ll b = n ;
        double a = x ;
        if(b < 0)
        {
            a = 1 / a ;
            b = -b ;
        }
        // step 2 : binary exponentiation
        double res = 1 ;
        while(b > 0)
        {
            if(b & 1)
            {
                res = res * a ;
            }
            a = a * a ;
            b >>= 1 ;
        }
        // step 3
        return res ;
    }
};
149. Max Points on a Line Hard

LeetCode · cf/Max_Points_on_a_Line.cpp

// orz
// 149. Max Points on a Line · LeetCode · Hard
//
// Question:
// given an array points where points[i] = [xi, yi] is a point on the X-Y plane,
// return the maximum number of points that lie on the same straight line.
// Example 1: points = [[1,1],[2,2],[3,3]]                     -> 3
// Example 2: points = [[1,1],[3,2],[5,3],[4,1],[2,3],[1,4]]   -> 4
// Constraints: 1 <= points.length <= 300, points[i].length == 2,
//              -10^4 <= xi, yi <= 10^4, all the points are unique
//
// 1. Mathematical statement
//    max over all lines L of |{ i : point i lies on L }|.
//    points j and k lie on the line through i iff (xj - xi) * (yk - yi) == (xk - xi) * (yj - yi).
//
// 2. Brute force
//    for every pair (i , j), walk all k and count the ones on line(i , j) with the
